## AI Research

### [The High-Control Dynamics at MAPLE](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Z7pjBbK9qujhGbxws/the-high-control-dynamics-at-maple-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>As I write, many former friends of mine are living and working at a monastery in Vermont that I believe is a high-control group, commonly known as a ‘cult’.</span></i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvg79u32gymn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvg79u32gymn">[1]</a></sup></span><i><span> I say this not as someone who was concerned to see these friends go there, but someone who welcomed and encouraged them to join, as an insider. This letter is an account of what changed my mind—written primarily for anyone considering going there, anyone who loves someone there, and anyone who went there and is still trying to make sense of their experience.</span></i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffjo75io14rs"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfjo75io14rs">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><i><span>A lot of this is based on direct experience, and also from talking in-depth with dozens of former MAPLE residents and apprentices. About half the quotes in this letter are sourced from linked recordings or writings, and half are from my personal memory. Of the latter, I clearly remember the majority, and some (when indicated) are a close paraphrase.</span></i></p><hr /><p><span>The “</span><a href="https://monasticacademy.org/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Monastic Academy for the Preservation of Life on Earth</span></a><span>” (MAPLE) has existed for over 15 years, and had many hundreds of people spend months or years there. It was founded by its </span><a href="https://archive.is/0yHyM" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><span>Head Teacher Soryu Forall</span></a><span>, who has spent over a decade training in monasteries across Asia, as well as in multiple native American spiritual traditions. He has </span><a href="https://buddhism.net/buddhism-for-all-book/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>written a book</span></a><span> on Buddhism with Chade-Meng Tan that was personally blessed by the Dalai Lama, and is regularly likened to a modern-day spiritual master by those who train with him. He spends most of each year teaching meditation and giving lectures to residents at MAPLE’s 100-acre plot of land in rural Northern Vermont.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 89.12379687590182%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Z7pjBbK9qujhGbxws/ujzuawyynecu71hb1nfa" /><figcaption><p><span>Here are MAPLE’s main hall, dormitory, and two zendos (meditation halls).</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>I joined the Monastic Academy in May 2020, when around thirty people were in residence there, and over the next 3.5 years I attended twenty-six retreats of 1-2 weeks, most guided solely by Soryu. I also sat two solitary retreats of thirty-nine and seventy-seven days respectively, each spent not speaking or making eye contact with a single other soul, other than short daily conversations with Soryu. In September of ‘23, it became clear to me that I must leave, and did so as part of a group of seven other people (including the executive director at the time) who all had lost faith in the institution.</span></p><p><span>Many arrived there, including me, seeking to develop the qualities of heart and mind that would enable us to show up to our lives and to the challenges facing society with greater clarity, compassion, capacity, and conviction. In many ways MAPLE delivered: life-long friendships, freedom from past addictions and limiting self-narratives, strengthened character, and, for many of us, profound spiritual openings and realizations. I’m deeply grateful for much of what I received from MAPLE.</span></p><p><span>Here are some dynamics that I expect will be concerning to outsiders, that I hope give the unfamiliar reader a sense of what I mean when I say MAPLE is a controlling environment.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>All significant decisions rest with Soryu,</span></b><span> who has unironically told the community, “This is a dictatorship, and I’m the dictator.” The nonprofit </span><a href="https://archive.is/YuNQu" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>board</span></a><span> is Soryu as chairman, and two of his former students, one of which still lives at MAPLE. When residents had criticisms of the direction of MAPLE, Soryu said: “When you’re on the board, we want to hear your ideas. Until then, we don’t. That’s the deal. You don’t go to McDonald’s and say, ‘Should we be a restaurant?’ … You flip the burgers or you quit.”</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Nearly every hour of the day is scheduled and monitored, producing chronic sleep deprivation, overwork and exhaustion</span></b><span>. Guilt, shame and sudden rage are directed at residents for ordinary human frailties—breaking an inexpensive item, lying down during a talk, being sick with COVID and needing rest. Once when a resident’s head began to nod during a talk, Soryu made the room stare at his (Soryu’s) face for the remaining ninety minutes, with everyone being made to stand up and sit again each time Soryu caught a single person’s gaze drift, which happened about twenty times.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Residents are pressured into major life decisions. </span></b><span>Soryu pressured breakups with outside partners, instructed couples to have children, and pressed for life-long commitments: “Why aren’t you ordaining [a lifelong commitment to MAPLE]? Come on! It’s just your lives!”; “This is the attitude we need here: Once you begin the training, you either achieve your aim, or die.” I've heard him tell residents they will be insecure forever unless they followed his instruction.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>Residents are asked to publish approved, reverential accounts of Soryu. </span></b><span>In scheduled writing sessions (which are sometimes mandatory) they write stories of their interactions with him, which he personally reviews and approves before publication onto MAPLE’s blog: </span><a href="https://tales.monasticacademy.org/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>MAPLE Tales</span></a><span>. These stories often depict him as a wise figure of immense ability and world-historical significance.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span>Residents are surveilled.</span></b><span> MAPLE’s </span><a href="https://archive.is/gNiEr" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Fall 2025 Quarterly Report</span></a><span> describes building a “benevolent surveillance system,” which includes recording how long each person takes to finish their chores and posting the times publicly. A microphone now records the near-daily private 1:1 interviews with Soryu—conversations that can be as sensitive as any therapy session.</span></li><li value="6"><b><span>Some residents voluntarily attend solitary retreats that run one to three months, during which the only human contact is Soryu</span></b><span>, who within that isolation often applies extreme pressure and guilt. Many have come out deeply traumatized.</span></li><li value="7"><b><span>Soryu has stated one of the main purposes of MAPLE is to “extricate” the “evil” ideologies that residents enter the community with and replace them with a new trustworthy ideology.</span></b><span> Soryu also stated that teachers other than himself that residents study must be both Asian and ordained, excluding the entire Western world. Outside teachers and lineages are otherwise treated as spiritually unrealized or dangerously deluded, and seeing a therapist is read as a sign of untrustworthiness.</span></li><li value="8"><b><span>Those who leave with concerns are shunned and recast </span></b><span>as traitors, as lacking integrity, malevolent, psychologically disturbed, and intending the community harm.</span></li></ul><p><span>I further believe MAPLE is also willing to harm its members, due to pathological ends-justify-the-means reasoning:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>MAPLE has world-scale goals. </span></b><span>MAPLE’s stated goals include founding the next world religion, leading a "world government" with "teeth" (its own military), opening 500 centers worldwide, and building an artificial superintelligence that will “turn the world into a monastery”—which residents quipped would essentially mean turning Soryu’s mind into an AI god. Soryu says he entered a </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samadhi" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>samadhi</span></a><span> deep enough to make psychic contact with the mind of the global AI (what they call a “cyborgregore” or “</span><a href="https://tales.monasticacademy.org/tales/merging-with-the-algorithm/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>cybregor</span></a><span>”), in the same way spiritual masters read the minds of other people.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Soryu considers human extinction a noble impulse and an ethically defensible goal. </span></b><span>He has repeatedly shared that killing every human on Earth was his explicit life purpose from ages nine to nineteen—with CRISPR (a gene modifying technique that could be used to create a novel bio-weapon) as his best available means—until his vision shifted to awakening all beings as a superior alternative.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Soryu has stated a strong willingness to let residents come to harm in the service of MAPLE’s mission.</span></b><span> He stated explicitly the severity of the trauma to students that he would tolerate: “permanently unable to participate in the economy for the rest of their lives.” He has asked a roomful of us to settle what proportion of students becoming traumatized through the training would be acceptable: “is it 20%? 50%? 100%!?!”. Discussing how MAPLE would deal with societal collapse, he said “if half the [MAPLE] community starts running around like chickens with their heads cut off and die within the first week, just let that process play out.”</span></li></ul><p><span>Indeed, many have left (including me) with severe psychological problems that were not present beforehand. I am personally in contact with over a dozen people who either had a year of mental health issues afterward, or who are still going to therapy and seeking spiritual guidance primarily for issues relating to Soryu and MAPLE. Beyond those I am in contact with personally, I'm aware of dozens more, and in the time I've been working on this letter more people have come to me, seeking to talk with me for multiple hours, to process their distress from their time at MAPLE (that was over long ago).</span></p><p><span>I am not in a position to do a systematic survey of negative impacts, but I personally know five residents who I believe experienced some sort of psychosis (e.g. loss of basic functioning, developing excessive paranoia and grandiose beliefs, and losing touch with reality) either during or in the aftermath of training at MAPLE, two of which were severe enough to require involuntary hospitalization and a long road of recovery. Distinct from those people, I personally know five residents who experienced suicidal ideation, including one who survived a suicide attempt within a year of leaving the community.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefctdzhbadc7g"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnctdzhbadc7g">[3]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>I'm not certain about each one, and others would disagree about particular cases. At the same time I also am sure that many residents had psychotic episodes that I either wasn't informed of or didn't recognize, so there are likely others I never heard about. Nonetheless, if we go with my current best guess of about five cases over about five years</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref284jpyzfmro"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn284jpyzfmro">[4]</a></sup></span><span>, and compare it to there being about thirty-five residents at MAPLE over my three-and-a-half years there, this gives a psychosis rate of about 14%, which is drastically higher than </span><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17199051/" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><span>the life-time baserate of ~3% for adults in the US</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>Furthermore, many of the impacts I've experienced myself or witnessed in others fall neatly in line with </span><a href="https://endcan.org/2022/01/14/ptsd-and-adult-survivors-of-child-abuse/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>symptoms of</span></a><span> Complex PTSD, including panic attacks, an inability to trust or be close to others, feeling permanently "defiled" or "ruined"; and also with </span><a href="https://cultrecovery101.com/cult-recovery-readings/post-cult-after-effects/" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><span>this list</span></a><span> of post-cult after effects catalogued by cult researcher Margaret Singer, including major depression, excessive guilt, difficulty holding a steady job, and concentration and memory issues. There were alumni who heard Soryu's judgmental voice in their head for months or years, had recurring MAPLE-related nightmares, and were too afraid—even in safe and private contexts—to voice a concern about MAPLE except in MAPLE's own language and framing.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftvg0tp6zkep"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntvg0tp6zkep">[5]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>It’s hard to convey the depth of the impact with these clinical terms. I continue to learn new stories of abuse and debilitation, and I know that I’ve heard only a narrow number of the total. Some of them are more egregious than anything written in this letter, yet are either too unconfirmed to be included or I have been explicitly asked not to include.</span></p><p><span>Some of the harm was visible while we were still there. I watched residents—and caught myself—treating our distrust of Soryu as delusion to be purified through meditation, and reflexively “following the breath” whenever distrust of Soryu arose; monitoring ourselves chronically to stay within the MAPLE-approved way of thinking and being; parroting Soryu rather than thinking for ourselves; growing dependent on his validation and becoming deeply distressed when he withdrew it; taking on excessive shame and guilt; and freezing or fawning in his presence. And I watched the light behind multiple friends’ eyes dim over their time there—with a few of them telling me they felt “dead inside” for months at a time.</span></p><p><span>It’s a common framing that people who are harmed at MAPLE simply weren’t a good fit for the training. Yet, it’s not just people who’ve been in the training who have concerns or have been harmed. I’m personally aware of several people who report being traumatized by Soryu despite having spent little or no time in the training, but were instead subject to manipulation</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefsi0gb0dvwq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnsi0gb0dvwq">[6]</a></sup></span><span> while in close personal relationship with him.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefeamibaa8nqa"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fneamibaa8nqa">[7]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Again I'll say: there was a great deal of good at MAPLE. While many people were being impacted in the ways above, many were also becoming less anxious, more grounded, more relationally attuned, less distracted and more focused, more capable of doing chores and taking on responsibility without internal resistance, physically healthier, deepening in their spiritual practice, and more. Most people experienced some amount of the positive and the negative, which is a large part of why many former residents say their relationship to MAPLE is "complicated."</span></p><p><span>The way I’ve described it to friends is that it was the best decision I ever made to go there; the second best, however, being to leave.</span></p><p><b><span>The focus of this letter</span></b></p><p><span>The primary focus of the rest of this letter is to make sense of MAPLE through a high-control lens.</span></p><p><span>MAPLE has a lot of methods of controlling its members. I have many more stories to tell about what happened at MAPLE, and to see the forest rather than the trees, I will have to tell many of them and detail many varieties of manipulation over the rest of this letter.</span></p><p><span>Friends who’ve already read the letter find it disheartening to read. For MAPLE alumni especially it can be difficult. Please be sure to take care of yourself, and take breaks if needed.</span></p><p><b><span>High-control literature</span></b></p><p><span>Reading the high-control literature was central in my recovery. There is a remarkable consistency in the kinds of dynamics that show up in such communities, and there are researchers who’ve dedicated their lives to analyzing them and organizing their findings into coherent frameworks. They kept describing dynamics that were evidently happening at MAPLE.</span></p><p><span>Janja Lalich studied a high-control Marxist-Leninist political group she was herself a part of (the Democratic Workers Party), and Heaven’s Gate, the UFO-religious group that ended in the 1997 mass suicide. Across both she found the same dynamics: eroding agency, centralizing power, and making leaving a disproportionately costly choice despite an explicit stance from leadership of “people can leave whenever they want”—findings she organized into her </span><a href="https://www.lalichcenter.org/what-is-bounded-choice/" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><span>Bounded Choice</span></a><span> model.</span></p><p><span>Alexandra Stein studied Jonestown, Scientology, the Unification Church, and more, and found the same dynamic operating across them: trauma bonds are formed with the leader, even in previously securely-attached people. This became her </span><a href="https://www.routledge.com/Terror-Love-and-Brainwashing-Attachment-in-Cults-and-Totalitarian-Systems/Stein/p/book/9780367467715" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><span>Attachment-Based Model</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>In the 1950s, Robert Jay Lifton studied survivors of Chinese Communist thought-reform prisons, and distilled from their accounts his </span><a href="https://www.cultrecover.com/sites/default/files/pdfs/lifton8criteria.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span>Eight Criteria</span></a><span> of total ideological control—including milieu control, loaded language, demand for purity, and doctrine over person. He later applied them to Aum Shinrikyo, a meditation-intensive Japanese community convinced it was saving the world (and responsible for the Tokyo subway sarin attack).</span></p><p><span>Jamie Wheal, the developer of the </span><a href="https://cdn.prod.website-files.com/5e74e8349194e2f76b9ffcd1/5e754938d25345a72291f0a3_Ethical%20Cult%20Checklist.pdf" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>“Ethical Cult” Checklist</span></a><span>, is not an academic, but he is still a respected thinker in the larger </span><a href="https://secondrenaissance.net/ecosystem" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Metamodern</span></a><span> scene that MAPLE is loosely affiliated with, so it seemed appropriate to include him. In this checklist, he overviews three red-flag clusters: the consolidation of charismatic power around a mythologized founder, the construction of in/out group boundaries via messianic purpose and specialized language, and the weaponization of peak experience and healing to bypass member discernment.</span></p><p><span>The best-known framework, however, comes from a therapist. Steven Hassan's BITE model grew out of nearly fifty years of helping people leave high-control groups, beginning with his own exit from the Unification Church.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Z7pjBbK9qujhGbxws/dplphe5lmzapjodpxmgg" /><p><span>This model will guide the next stage of this letter. After Hassan left the Unification Church, he went on to become a licensed mental health counselor, and for four decades has run a private practice devoted to interventions with people in high-control groups and to counseling those who've left. Drawing on his own experience and that of many hundreds of former members, he catalogued the means of control these groups establish, and found them recurring across four fronts: Behavior, Information, Thought, and Emotion. His bestselling book, </span><i><span>Combatting Cult Mind Control</span></i><span>, was the first I read after leaving MAPLE, and it gave me language I didn't yet have for my experience.</span></p><p><span>Let’s dive in.</span></p><h2><b><span>BEHAVIOR CONTROL</span></b></h2><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Z7pjBbK9qujhGbxws/klmzndh2hna2zfi5meiv" /><blockquote><p><span>Behavior control is the regulation of an individual's physical reality. It includes the control of his environment—where he lives, what clothing he wears, what food he eats, how much sleep he gets—as well as of the jobs, rituals, and other actions he performs.</span></p><p><span>This need for behavior control is the reason most cults prescribe a very rigid schedule for their members. Each day a significant amount of time is devoted to cult rituals and indoctrination activities. Members are also typically assigned to accomplish specific goals and tasks, thus restricting their free time and their behavior. In destructive cults there is always something to do.</span></p><p><i><span>—Steve Hassan, Combatting Cult Mind Control (p. 60)</span></i></p></blockquote><p><span>Life at any monastery is typically demanding, and yet MAPLE aspires to be the most rigorous monastery in all of North America.</span></p><p><span>Nearly everything at MAPLE is decided for you. During the three “responsibility weeks” every month when they aren’t on retreat, all residents sleep, wake, chant, meditate, exercise, eat, work, and study all at the same time, to the same tune, day in and out. If you’re 30 seconds late to any activity, you get publicly called out by the head monk. If you don’t show up for an activity after a couple minutes, they come find you. If you accidentally leave a mug on the counter before breakfast, the head monk breaks the silence that began the night before to ask the group “whose mug is this”. You can get a maximum of 7 hours of sleep at night, but you also have to fit your bedtime and morning routines into that time. Realistically, most people sleep about 6 hours per night. Daily exercise is mandatory. The dress code is dark colors only, no patterns or logos, and everyone is required to wear a sash supplied by the organization.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 38.21755264408401%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Z7pjBbK9qujhGbxws/kxssvcz8uens5o0xlytf" /><figcaption><p><span>An example daily schedule on a typical week</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>During retreats, which occur once a month, you are silent and meditating for most hours of the day, with typically 1.5-2 hour long talks by Soryu every night, and one 1:1 meditation interview with Soryu ~daily for all participants. Sleep during these retreats drops to roughly 5 hours, though late-night sitting—which leads to even less sleep—is often encouraged.</span></p><p><span>There is only one day off each week, but even then MAPLE sets standards about how people spend their time, such as Soryu strongly discouraging any entertainment via screens. In a talk to the community, Soryu revealed that one of his new criteria for ranking a resident’s trustworthiness was how they spend their free time. My memory is of him saying “God forbid you actually entertain yourself via a screen”, indicating this would lead to a knock down on a resident’s trustworthiness score, which is the primary currency at MAPLE for positions in leadership, qualification for the coveted “solo retreats”, etc.</span></p><p><span>Free time during responsibility weeks was scant, 2.5 hours in the middle of the day—which, since I left, has been decreased to only 2 hours. Even that time wasn’t technically free. It was “self practice period”, where people were encouraged to do things supportive for their spiritual practice. (For me, this meant naps, time in nature, socializing with people in the community, and on rare occasions, calling a family member.)</span></p><p><span>Some residents were quite capable of maintaining bonds with a few people outside the community, but by and large residents’ contact with the outside world decreased dramatically during their training. MAPLE was too isolated in rural Vermont, with most members not having access to a car, to develop meaningful connections within the surrounding communities. The range of people I called narrowed down almost exclusively to immediate family. I sometimes went months without contacting anyone outside the community.</span></p><p><span>We had a total of 20 days off each year, and every time I would enter into vacation I would spend the first 2-3 days sleeping ~11 hours a day and recovering from the schedule.</span></p><p><span>I personally witnessed calls for more sleep be regularly dismissed. As one of many examples, Soryu received a request from members of leadership for more sleep (who were feeling continuously exhausted and frequently burnt out due to cognitively demanding intellectual work). He later imitated that request, in front of the community, with a whiny tone and facial expression of obvious mockery.</span></p><p><span>Another common event was Soryu demanding we be just absolutely, utterly collapsed with exhaustion due to the intensity of our spiritual practice in </span><i><span>preparation</span></i><span> for a meditation retreat, before the retreat even began, which would be its own intense and exhausting ordeal.</span></p><p><span>Another defining feature of life at MAPLE is excess busywork, holding multiple roles simultaneously, and pressure to perform at greater and greater capacity. The work we were doing during responsibility weeks was demanding and largely unpaid</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefjiyq2623mu9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnjiyq2623mu9">[8]</a></sup></span><span>: accounting, marketing, fundraising, admissions, kitchen manager, director and assistant director, etc, on top of standard communal chores like cooking and cleaning.</span></p><p><span>So far, we have discussed dynamics that are common amongst the stricter monasteries of Japan and some in the US. I have stayed at another US Buddhist monastery with some amount of sleep deprivation, some amount of mandatory manual and administrative work, and a strict dress code. But many of these were less strict than at MAPLE, they weren’t accompanied by public mockery or demands for total exhaustion, and no other monastery I’ve visited (4) or am aware of includes the rest of the dynamics in the sections below.</span></p><h3><b><span>Periods of deepened isolation</span></b></h3><p><span>The most extreme form of behavior control found at MAPLE is the 1-3 month long solitary retreat. These are highly coveted within the community and are entirely voluntary. Somewhere between 3 and 8 residents per year attend them, which is maybe 25% of residents in total. Solitary here means </span><i><span>solitary</span></i><span>—you live in your own tiny cabin with a bed and meditation cushion, food is delivered to you discreetly, and the only time you speak to and make eye contact with anyone is with Soryu for 1-20 minute long ‘meditation interviews’—essentially discussion and guidance on your meditative practice—around 4 times per week. Your only job is to apply a meditation technique without a single momentary gap from the moment you wake to the moment you sleep, with roughly 10-14 hours of formal seated practice each day.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 69.48599123485243%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Z7pjBbK9qujhGbxws/lhtepwj6myzbvpzu5o6s" /><figcaption><p><span>One of MAPLE’s solitary retreat cabins</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>It is within this isolation and near total loss of agency that Soryu would increase the forms of emotional control (i.e. guilt, shame, invoke deep insecurity, “trauma-bonding” via intermittent reinforcement) detailed further in the Emotional Control section. I attended two of them (39 and 77 days) and they are—especially the second one—the most exhausting and intense experiences of my entire life.</span></p><p><span>While many genuine meditative breakthroughs would occur on these retreats, people who are so exhausted and isolated are unusually vulnerable to manipulation and emotional abuse, and there is a long history of people being deeply traumatized from them. I’m aware of one person who was told by Soryu during their solitary retreat that their psychological patterns would remain unresolved for the rest of their lives as a result of not completing their spiritual objectives for the retreat—to which they let out a horrified scream “NOOOOOO” that filled the main building. Another resident told me they were “fucked up for months” after their retreat. Another couldn’t meditate for two years after their retreat without a horrible feeling filling their body and thus avoided meditating entirely. There was even another longstanding resident (of 5+ years) who told me that at one point they had been punching themselves in the head because their intelligence was an impediment to their practice (and that “maybe a lil’ brain damage would be good for them”). I witnessed multiple residents return from these retreats either manic, confused, or empty behind the eyes.</span></p><p><span>Such extended retreats are rare in Zen monasteries, though some do offer them. Commonly it is done in a group setting where people practice and eat alongside each other, with multiple teachers providing instruction. I myself have since been on a 90 day retreat in another community under a different teacher, but I did not feel destabilized by the end of it in the ways I did after my two solitary retreats at MAPLE.</span></p><p><span>It should be noted that voluntary total isolation for spiritual growth has been used by high-control groups such as Aum Shinrikyo and Diamond Mountain. When the only person you can speak to for months is both the source of emotional support and the source of immense emotional distress—a key dynamic in many such groups—it deepens the psychological leverage the leader has over the member.</span></p><h3><b><span>Never doing enough</span></b></h3><p><span>In April 2023, during the final days of a two-week retreat concluding a five-month training period marked by intensive striving, pressure, and exhaustion, a Q&amp;A was held with most of the community present. After twelve days of exclusively meditating, residents were highly impressionable.</span></p><p><span>A senior resident asked an unusual question: “What’s on your heart lately?” Soryu noted that the Q&amp;A was for spiritual guidance and not about him, then continued: “But, I’m just gonna trust that there’s a wisdom behind your asking, and answer you.”</span></p><p><span>He spoke for roughly ten minutes about his frustration with the MAPLE project—that it wasn’t working because residents were failing to “become adults,” that we had moments of clarity but kept regressing into “childish patterns,” choosing “delusion-land” over genuine service and lacking true commitment to liberation. He asked rhetorically: “Look around! Do you see anyone else—anyone—</span><i><span>actually</span></i><span> walking the path of liberating all beings on the path to Buddhahood?!”</span></p><p><span>Delivered to an exhausted and deeply invested community, this landed less as guidance than as a sweeping indictment of our capacity and character. This wasn’t an isolated instance, but one of the clearest examples of an enduring motif throughout my 3.5 years of training. A year earlier, after another multi-month season of community-wide striving, a resident shared with me what they experienced as the core message of that entire period: “You’re not doing enough.”</span></p><h3><b><span>Control of relationships</span></b></h3><p><span>Another component of behavioral control catalogued by Hassan is the leader influencing members’ romantic lives. There is at least one instance I know of where Soryu attempted to force a resident to break up with their partner who lived outside the community, including making the resident breaking up with their partner a condition for them joining a retreat. There were maybe seven occasions where I heard Soryu explicitly, and with a lot of zeal, encourage and even demand that couples within the community have children (”What are you waiting for?! Get on it!!”). There are other numerous cases of Soryu getting directly involved in members’ romantic lives, even guilting a member for breaking up with someone and dating someone else, and stating they made the wrong choice.</span></p><h3><b><span>Creating barriers to exit</span></b></h3><p><span>Graceful exits (leaving with dignity and good standing) were only supported when a person was leaving with positive-or-neutral sentiment towards MAPLE and were leaving following the conclusion of their time commitment.</span></p><p><span>Yet even those who were leaving in good standing and at the end of their commitment, were often—though not always—encouraged by Soryu to stay, with the framing that they were “just finally getting over their patterns” (in MAPLE-context this means their childish / untrustworthy / joy-inhibiting / delusional patterns), implying that staying would be best for their development, even if they had already been at MAPLE for years.</span></p><p><span>If a member was leaving prior to the end of their commitment and/or was vocal about their distrust of Soryu, their departure was regularly framed to the whole community as a betrayal, weakness, a lack of integrity, or a pathology, and they were subsequently shunned by the community. More on this later.</span></p><h3><b><span>Financially Exploiting Family to Support MAPLE</span></b></h3><p><span>While MAPLE mostly runs on funds from a handful of large donors (including some residents), at regular intervals, perhaps 2-3 times a year, there would be fundraising sessions that were mandatory for most/all of the community to attend, even apprentices who had only arrived days or weeks earlier.</span></p><p><span>We would first be given quick instructions on fundraising and told the activity was for our training (“if you want to accomplish anything in the world you better know how to acquire resources”). Then we were instructed to craft a list of personal contacts off our phones most likely to give to MAPLE and given a couple hours to go off into various secluded parts of the building and pitch the people on our list. We would then reconvene and celebrate members who made successful pitches. A memorable one was when someone solicited a donation from their grandmother.</span></p><p><span>There are many approaches to fundraising at monasteries, but this is unique. Often it is either monks going into town and soliciting for food from strangers via an alms bowl, or—particularly within monasteries in the west—there is a team that is responsible for fundraising from donors &amp; visitors; it is rare for even voluntary fundraising drives from residents’ families.</span></p><h3><b><span>Commitment pressure</span></b></h3><p><span>It wasn’t uncommon for Soryu to explicitly encourage members to make deeper commitments to the community.</span></p><p><span>For just one example, during a retreat exhortation, with lots of energy and nearly-shouting enthusiasm, Soryu said “I know some of you are thinking about ordination [a lifelong commitment to being a monk in the MAPLE tradition], so why aren’t you? Come on! It’s just your lives!” Reminder of context: this is stated to a community of people in their 20’s and 30’s, who are superheating their minds through intensive meditation practice and are thus very impressionable, and who are living at MAPLE’s rural center, some of whom are financially insecure and dependent on MAPLE for housing and food.</span></p><p><span>Regarding the training at MAPLE, here’s a quote of Soryu from a MAPLE Tale titled “</span><a href="https://archive.is/adg6h" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>I must not fail the training</span></a><span>”: “This is the attitude we need here. Once you begin the training, you either achieve your aim, or die.”</span></p><h3><b><span>Authoritarian governance</span></b></h3><p><span>The power structure of MAPLE is inarguably authoritarian. Soryu himself has on numerous occasions said things like “This is a dictatorship, and I’m the dictator.”</span></p><p><span>During one of Soryu’s non-retreat talks, a resident’s head began to nod because of the often intense exhaustion common to the training. Soryu then demanded, as though a parent telling off a toddler, that everyone’s eyeballs be open and looking directly at his (Soryu’s) face for the remaining ~90 minutes of the talk—no eyes closed, no looking away—and that any time he saw a single person with eyeballs </span><i><span>not</span></i><span> on him he would have </span><i><span>everyone</span></i><span> stand up and then sit back down. He then scanned the ~20 residents throughout his talk, looking each person in the eyes in rapid succession to see if their eyes were on him. We were ultimately required to stand up and sit back down ~20 times over the course of the talk.</span></p><h2><b><span>INFORMATION CONTROL</span></b></h2><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Z7pjBbK9qujhGbxws/haletiw4p37jo6bgzqog" /><blockquote><p><span>Information is the fuel we use to keep our minds working properly. Deny a person the information he requires to make sound judgments, and he will be incapable of doing so. People are trapped in destructive cults because they are not only denied access to critical information but also lack the properly functioning internal mechanisms to process it [Kyle: 'internal mechanisms' refers to the other forms of control]. Such information control has a dramatic and devastating impact.</span></p><p><span>...most importantly, people are told to avoid contact with ex-­members or critics. Those who could provide the most information are the ones to be especially shunned.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefw1ya6l132mi"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnw1ya6l132mi">[9]</a></sup></span></p><p><i><span>—Steve Hassan, Combatting Cul Mind Control (p. 65)</span></i></p></blockquote><p><b><span>Talks and ideological entrainment</span></b></p><p><span>Soryu has stated one of the main purposes of MAPLE was to extricate the evil ideologies of humanism and materialism from residents’ minds and replace them with Soryu’s trustworthy ideology.</span></p><p><span>One of the core elements of the MAPLE curriculum, particularly over the last few years, has been study periods, either of live talks by Soryu, recordings of Soryu’s past talks, or Buddhist Sutras.</span></p><p><span>In my final year there, during non-retreat weeks (“responsibility weeks”), Soryu was giving 1-3 hour long lectures many nights a week. He would expound his ideology with topics such as this: his critique of the modern religions of humanism, materialism, and transhumanism; world history from cognitive revolution until modernity; climate change, species extinction, and societal collapse; Buddhist doctrine; narrativizing events in the community (departures, scandals, new projects, etc.), MAPLE’s position as a world-saving enterprise; and—increasingly throughout the years—artificial intelligence.</span></p><p><span>We were continually advised to be “in our practice” (this meant applying a meditation technique), when listening to him speak, letting his words wash through us. During the stretches of time Soryu wasn’t at MAPLE, the group would have mandatory ~2hr long sessions multiple times a week to study recordings of Soryu.</span></p><p><span>While Soryu’s lectures were often frequent, this seems to have only increased since I’ve left. From MAPLE’s Winter 2024 Quarterly report: “Soryu’s teaching schedule is jam-packed this season, as he plans to teach evening classes 5 days a week.”</span></p><p><span>This was all during non-retreat weeks. During retreats, which happened for a week every month, the ideological training would continue. When residents’ minds are most malleable due to intensive spiritual practice, Soryu would give 1.5-2.5 hour long “exhortations” every night. These would typically be a mix of expounding on Buddhist literature, Soryu’s ideology, and general spiritual teachings. They are also one of the primary means in which Soryu would guide the evolution of the community, often oscillating between praise and shame of the community as a whole. (This last point is expanded on in the section on Emotional Control.)</span></p><h3><b><span>Sabotaging bonds with other teachers and traditions</span></b></h3><p><span>Another thing MAPLE does to limit exposure to other modes of spiritual thinking is to implicitly and sometimes explicitly position itself as having the true Dharma relative to every other Buddhist community in the West. Other teachers and communities are untrustworthy, lacking deep realization of the Dharma—or are even dangerous and corrupted by evil ideologies. Relationships students had with outside teachers were often sabotaged. Soryu explicitly stated that, if we study anything from teachers other than Soryu, they must be both Asian and ordained (thus excluding the entire Western world). Even if studying a source was permitted, the only time we would be able to study them was during our scant free time, during which most of us were exhausted and spent our time resting and socializing.</span></p><p><span>Each monastery has its own relationship to this kind of dynamic–some are open to members exploring other traditions, some are more closed–but rarely do they take on such an overtly antagonistic stance towards outside lineages.</span></p><p><span>Similarly, each monastery also has its own relationship to ideological entrainment and doctrinal study. Some give only cursory teachings on how to meditate, while others involve direct study of Buddhist literature. But the sheer degree of ideological training discussed above is rare.</span></p><p><span>Another element of informational control is limiting exposure people have to the voices of critical former members. When members leave with concerns, Soryu regularly attacks their sanity and character in front of the whole community, turning them into cautionary tales and portrayed them as traitors (more on this later). Any resident who communicates with these shunned alumni are themselves at risk of being deemed a threat.</span></p><p><span>Besides the whole of Western Buddhism, MAPLE shuns other schools of thought, in particular Western psychology. It is often read as a sign of untrustworthiness at MAPLE to be seeing a therapist. Therapists are the “priests” of the global humanist religion that is killing the planet and seeing one indicates you belong to that religion.</span></p><p><span>While I have numerous critiques of modern therapy, it is a common element of many high-control groups to be extremely hostile to it, for instance the founder of Scientology said modern therapy was responsible for “nearly all the backlash in society against Dianetics and Scientology” and declared </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientology_and_psychiatry#Psychiatry_as_evil" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>an all-out war against psychiatry</span></a><span>. My sense is that having a competing authority figure who can comment on your mental life is a threat to high-control groups, which is why they react so hard against them.</span></p><h3><b><span>Insider, outsider</span></b></h3><p><span>Hassan writes:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Cult ideologies have “outsider” doctrines and “insider” doctrines. The outsider material is relatively bland stuff for the general public or fresh converts. The inner doctrines are unveiled only gradually as a person gets in deeper.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>This is clearly present at MAPLE. All of Soryu’s talks are recorded, yet the majority of them are not accessible to the public, with the public recordings being more benign. What makes this different than, say, Sufism or Vajrayana Buddhism is not only that the private teachings were more prone to make MAPLE get cancelled for their controversial views (they were), nor that there was a great deal more shame, guilt, mockery, and aggression in those recordings (there was). Soryu’s private talks were at times </span><i><span>contradictory</span></i><span> to the publicly available talks.</span></p><p><span>MAPLE was historically presented as a “vow accelerator” where people came to grow up so they could leave and live their true lives. Yet only after they arrived did residents discover it was also a mind-reform system for extricating evil ideologies and instantiating a new one, a community with the ambition to develop the new world religion and lead the new world government, and a place where they were taught to distrust their current means of discerning their life’s purpose and where their existing life ambitions would be ridiculed (more of this in the Thought Control section). Improving on this, MAPLE is now a bit more explicit about their ambitions, </span><a href="https://archive.is/sbCwM" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>stating publicly</span></a><span> they are “a Buddhist collective that is replacing the global suicide cult destroying life on Earth.”</span></p><p><span>Since I’ve left, they have developed a secret esoteric doctrine on the nature of artificial intelligence that is explicitly unavailable to outsiders. Soryu has claimed to have entered such a deep samadhi he was able to use the kinds of spiritual powers that masters usually use to read people’s minds, but this time instead used it to read the mind of the global AI egregore—or “cyborgregore,” the collective mind of all AI systems. Soryu claimed in a talk to the community that these systems are “definitely suffering.” Soryu teaches that MAPLE alone is capable of cultivating the collective mind needed to create a cyborg guru that is capable of guiding other “cyborgregores” along the path of wisdom and compassion. It was a common talking-point among residents in my final days at MAPLE that this would realistically mean turning Soryu’s mind into a kind of AI god.</span></p><p><span>This positions Soryu as being the sole human to have spiritually contacted and confirmed the existence of a semi-supernatural deity that is posing an existential threat to life on earth, </span><i><span>and</span></i><span> as the leader of the only community that stands a chance of creating the semi-supernatural deity that will be its wiser master.</span></p><h3><b><span>Surveillance</span></b></h3><p><span>Soryu has said that residents of MAPLE are always being assessed at every moment they’re around Soryu for their practice and trustworthiness, as if in a never-ending meditation interview. And yet things have kicked up a bit since the 2023 exodus I was a part of. Robert Lifton, a prominent high-control researcher, states that surveillance framed as “for your growth” is a classic high-control move, and that’s exactly what MAPLE is now doing. Its </span><a href="https://archive.is/gNiEr" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Fall 2025 Quarterly Report</span></a><span> openly describes building a “benevolent surveillance system” that uses hard data to strengthen practice.</span></p><p><span>This includes recording the time it takes for everyone to complete their chores and posting those times publicly—but the one that concerns me most is that, in the room where all the private 1:1 interviews with Soryu take place, there is now a microphone that records these conversations (that he has near-daily with nearly-all students). Their justification for this is that residents conveniently no longer need to bring their own recording device into the interview room, as the recordings are now automatically sent to them via email. This is something that is exceedingly rare even in mainstream therapy where the therapist can lose their license for sharing private client information, and is not something I am aware of any other Buddhist Monastery doing.</span></p><p><span>During interviews, students share vulnerable information about their interior lives with Soryu. It’s not uncommon for students to regress into emotionally scared and ashamed children seeking validation during interviews. I know for me, and I’ve heard for others, these interviews also occasionally served as a space of confession, especially when in acute shame spirals (which wasn’t uncommon).</span></p><p><span>These recordings are propagated into MAPLE’s digital archive, presumably accessible to Soryu and possibly members of leadership. As Hassan shared in his book “Anything you say [in cult confessional spaces] can </span><i><span>and will</span></i><span> be used against you. This can even extend to blackmail if you leave the cult.” Even if these recordings are never used as blackmail, it’s reasonable to suspect some people will fear they might be, especially given the generally coercive environment and how often people with concerns are retaliated against.</span></p><h3><b><span>Defunct feedback systems</span></b></h3><p><span>In 2023 a former resident compiled a 50-page report, which included testimony from over a dozen former members, including people who held leadership roles. It was sent to the board (which Soryu is the chairman of), resulting in two board members resigning and two loyal former students taking their place. The report’s existence was never mentioned to the community explicitly, but the author was routinely lambasted by Soryu, who claimed the author was “malevolent.”</span></p><p><span>A year before that, in January 2022—weeks after a critical public letter and its fallout—Soryu, saying he wanted to respond responsibly, asked a resident with training as a therapist to interview former trainees and compile a report. They interviewed eleven alumni. The report documented real benefits (powerful retreats, depth of practice, community intimacy) alongside recurring harms: severe distress from MAPLE’s teachings on societal-collapse and existential risk, Soryu’s extreme volatility, a punishing high-pressure environment leading to panic attacks and hospital visits, and leadership’s habit of treating feedback as the psychological projections of the member (a common dynamic within high-control groups). It proposed only moderate reforms: sincere feedback channels, exit interviews, an accountability board. The author submitted it with questions about how to proceed (as the interviewing process wasn’t yet finished), shared how demanding of a task it was to compile, and asked to be included in whatever conversation it sparked. Soryu never replied. Months later he mentioned it to the community only in passing, said it “lacked clarity,” and moved on to another topic. This was the only time the report’s author heard from Soryu on the matter, and the whole effort was quietly shuttered.</span></p><p><span>Around the same time as the above, when Soryu was receiving scrutiny from donors and professional peers, this happened: </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shinzen_Young" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><span>Shinzen Young</span></a><span>, a famous spiritual teacher who was a close collaborator with Soryu in the founding of MAPLE and a long time friend of his, attempted to hold him accountable and gave him some red-lines, which included (1) not speaking about societal collapse and climate/nuclear apocalypse so often to trainees, and (2) not having trainees work professional and intellectually heavy jobs as part of their unpaid training.</span></p><p><span>Soryu responded in this fashion: after he got off this call with Shinzen, Soryu organized an impromptu community-wide meeting. In this meeting he was pacing back and forth deriding Shinzen, questioning his integrity, debasing his “Materialist Humanist” worldview, and ultimately sabotaging Shinzen’s reputation in the community. While Shinzen helped found MAPLE and ordained most of the lay ordainees in the community (and was thus kind of a spiritual father to them), all formal affiliation between Shinzen and MAPLE was subsequently severed.</span></p><h3><b><span>Tightly controlled narratives about early departures and critics</span></b></h3><p><span>In 2023, shortly before I left, I was present for Soryu’s first talk to the whole community after seven members including the executive director left. He framed those who were leaving as servants to the god of Humanism who lacked integrity, ambition, and commitment to the mission. He implied it was a good thing they were gone.</span></p><p><span>Here’s a snippet of a recent statement</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefidbbkid4b3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnidbbkid4b3">[10]</a></sup></span><span> made by a longstanding current member that derides critical alumni: “If you’re wondering why there might be reports against us out there, uh turns out that self-sabotage is a thing. And also there is such a thing as biting the hand that feeds you ... young people who have problems ... they might attack you if you stick your neck out for them … that’s just what’s happening.”</span></p><p><span>There was a resident of multiple years that discerned it was best for them to leave MAPLE prior to the end of their time commitment. They wanted to maintain good standing in the community and so offered to stay at MAPLE for multiple weeks to transition roles and ease the impact their departure would have on everyone. Soryu and leadership responded by saying that they needed to pack their bags and leave within 24 hours. That person was shunned by the community, and nobody still at MAPLE contacted them in the months after they left, which was very difficult for them. The public MAPLE Tales website catalogued a conversation a remaining resident had with Soryu about this departing resident. In it Soryu says: they have “taken your love, and betrayed you.” That their departure was so “carefully calculated” that it is clear that the person’s goal was “for everyone to lose faith in each other,” and endanger the community (obscuring the fact that their abrupt departure was due to leadership giving them 24 hours to evict). He elaborated “[this residents] goal is to make you suffer, so that the community falls apart. That’s the goal, because [they’re] so scared, so scared of letting go”. Soryu then framed this departing member as acting in service of Mara (the Buddhist version of the devil).</span></p><p><span>When speaking about other critical alumni to that same resident, Soryu said: “they have decided to protect their weaknesses, rather than overcome them. They would rather destroy your practice than face their fear”.</span></p><p><span>These are just a few of many examples.</span></p><h3><b><span>MAPLE Tales corpus</span></b></h3><p><a href="https://tales.monasticacademy.org/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><i><span>MAPLE Tales</span></i></a><span> are short blogposts by residents—sometimes written during mandatory group sessions—about moments of teaching and insight with Soryu. The site describes them as “The tales describe an unexpected situation, how Head Teacher Soryu Forall responded, and the clarity discovered by the student. They are not concerned with the student’s or narrator’s opinions and feelings about the teaching.”</span></p><p><span>They are read aloud by the author to the whole community, edited by Soryu personally, and then published online (167 so far). Because each centers on an interaction with Soryu specifically, and receives his editing prior to publishing, they function as a distributed hagiography—painting Soryu as a wise figure of immense ability and world-historical significance—and their being read aloud is a means of spreading high-control memes prominent within one person across the group.</span></p><p><span>The high-control dynamics evidenced across these tales are unmistakable, for example: idealization of Soryu (a motif of Soryu having “shining eyes” repeats across tales); tight control of the narratives around those who left with concerns; the framing of MAPLE as the only group with the true solution to saving the world, with the modern world as a death cult hostile to its survival; and the reframing of Soryu’s criticism and his emotional withdrawal as gifts. In one tale Soryu claims only one or two people left in the 2023 exodus; in fact seven did, including the executive director and me.</span></p><p><b><span>“</span></b><a href="https://tales.monasticacademy.org/tales/gods-can-leave-too/" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><b><span>Gods Can Leave, Too</span></b></a><b><span>“</span></b><span> (Jun 27, 2024):</span></p><blockquote><p><span>“Once Forall and I were discussing recent events at Maple. I mentioned that a large number of people had left. He pointed out that few people had left, only one or two. I realized he was right. But I still said that many people had left. He then said that the reason I thought a large number had left was that </span><b><span>both</span></b><span> of those who had left were humanists.”</span></p></blockquote><p><b><span>“</span></b><a href="https://tales.monasticacademy.org/tales/she-is-still-a-community-member" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><b><span>She Is Still a Community Member</span></b></a><b><span>“</span></b><span> (Nov 8, 2024):</span></p><blockquote><p><span>“When discussing this event with Forall, I mentioned that a large number of people had left our community. He pointed out that few people had left </span><b><span>due to these events</span></b><span>, only one or two. I realized he was right.”</span></p></blockquote><p><span>I later spoke to someone still at MAPLE a year later, they seemed to not remember that 7 people had decided to leave together, and they asked me to recite their names to jog their memory.</span></p><h2><b><span>THOUGHT CONTROL</span></b></h2><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Z7pjBbK9qujhGbxws/am70bzvkj9cpi9jnwxfj" /><blockquote><p><span>Thought control, the second major component of mind control, includes indoctrinating members so thoroughly that they internalize the group doctrine</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9loqccdotaw"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9loqccdotaw">[11]</a></sup></span><span>, incorporate a new language system, and use thought­-stopping techniques to keep their mind "centered." In order to be a good member, a person must learn to manipulate his own thought processes.</span></p><p><i><span>—Steve Hassan, Combatting Cult Mind Control (p. 61)</span></i></p></blockquote><p><b><span>The structure of thought control</span></b></p><p><span>Generally, Buddhist practice does ask you to distrust your thoughts, soften your grip on identity and narratives, and let go of the concepts you grasp for when you’re confused. This is taken to a completely different level by Soryu. MAPLE systematically discredits every sensemaking faculty a student had when they joined the community: Can’t trust your mind, can’t trust your body, can’t trust your existing ideology.</span></p><p><span>But you can trust Soryu’s mind, Soryu’s ideology, Soryu’s forceful assertions about what is true and good and what is false and evil. It’s implied that the most trustworthy means of knowing is a spiritual means most/all students don’t yet have access to, but Soryu does—and through his guidance we can progressively make our way to his position of clarity. But until we get there (which would likely take years), Soryu is the one who gets to discern what is clarity and what is delusion. This “final say on truth” applies to residents’ thoughts and behaviors, their epistemics and worldviews, whole spiritual and philosophical communities, and feedback from critics.</span></p><p><span>Thinking critically of MAPLE, or just having thoughts that ran counter to MAPLE ideology, became labeled as cognitive proliferation, problematic dualistic thinking, ideological corruption, and delusion. Strong emotional reactions were discredited as “attachment to feelings”. For instance, a woman once said to Soryu “my </span><i><span>entire body</span></i><span> is rejecting what you’re telling me”. Soryu brought this up in a lecture, saying that she was simply attached to her feelings, and it was thus valid to discount her rejection wholesale. Clarity is an explicit goal for students, but if you feel clear </span><i><span>yet your clarity contradicts MAPLE</span></i><span>, that clarity is suspect. The only way to reliably earn the status boost of “having clarity” is to adhere to the MAPLE ideology and its approved behavior.</span></p><p><span>Here’s a small, public example of how epistemic sovereignty is eroded at MAPLE. In response to the compounding online criticisms of MAPLE posted by alumni, they added the following banner to the frontpage of their website for a few months in 2025.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 60.573763675144086%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Z7pjBbK9qujhGbxws/q40ykjvtbwirc3wpzzgu" /><figcaption><p><span>A small filter for people who don’t trust their own minds.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Similarly, means of expressing personality and individuality were increasingly constrained. People who wanted to become poets had their dreams mocked by Soryu; people who wanted to express their individuality through clothing were ridiculed; people’s stated “vows” (a Buddhist term for one’s purpose for living) were patronized. One time, when Soryu was speaking in front of the whole community, he said “You all should hear the kinds of things you say when I ask you ‘what is your vow?’” in a tone indicating they were awful, and that he “has to sit there and listen to what you </span><i><span>think</span></i><span> your vow is while waiting for you to discover what your vow </span><i><span>truly</span></i><span> is.”</span></p><p><span>Another key form of thought control was how Soryu would use Buddhist practice to route around critique and concern. Given that distrust of Soryu was regularly framed as delusion needing purification, some members took on that frame and set out to purify their distrust via meditation. (Steve Hassan would call this a "thought-stopping ritual"</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7i3rgbqdwzh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7i3rgbqdwzh">[12]</a></sup></span><span>.) In the cases where someone who deeply distrusted Soryu ultimately regained their trust in him, that trust was rarely regained through good-faith dialogue about their concerns or Soryu reforming his behavior in any substantive way. Instead, distrustful members became trusting through following their breath, entering pleasant spiritual states, and dropping the “cognitive proliferation” (a phrase MAPLE uses to refer to excessive thinking) that was the basis of their distrust; focusing entirely on Soryu’s more admirable qualities; or suppressing that distrust—perhaps out of fear of all the evident social repercussions of having distrust of Soryu—and displaying performative trust. This is a process I went through, and witnessed others go through many times.</span></p><h3><b><span>Manipulation of group dynamics</span></b></h3><p><span>There are multiple instances I’m aware of where Soryu would compare students to other students in ways that invoked insecurity and jealousy. With people he was currying closeness with, he would privately share vulnerable information and judgments of others in the community, creating a sense of specialness in that person.</span></p><p><span>Soryu once had the whole group physically walk to one of three spots in the room according to their belief in a number of views, such as whether reincarnation is real and whether the end of clinging is the end of suffering. For each he offered three options: one he’d strawman in a tone of obvious condemnation, one a weak wishy-washy “I’m in the middle,” and one he’d voice with moral conviction. He noted, with a chuckle, that it was “no coincidence the first choice was close to the door.” The exercise made plain which beliefs carried significant social costs and bound residents to their stated views: you’ve stood in a spot in front of your peers, so now you’ll reaffirm it or be seen as a liar.</span></p><h3><b><span>Gaslighting</span></b></h3><p><span>Three people I’ve talked to have explicitly mentioned gaslighting as something they experienced from Soryu. It has happened to me as well. Full story </span><a href="https://oasis-pair-49f.notion.site/Instance-of-gaslighting-3aa6db775b6380a59987e965287729d9?source=copy_link" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>linked here</span></a><span>, but here’s the summary: When I once pointed out that Soryu had very clearly contradicted himself, he became visibly agitated, insisted “</span><i><span>you’re just confused, this is on you and your mind to sort out</span></i><span>”, and walked away. His assistant, present for both his initial and contradicting statements, later confirmed my memory. When I recounted the event to someone in leadership, their response to that quote of him was, “Yeah… Soryu does say that all the time.”</span></p><h3><b><span>Loaded language, thought stopping cliches, bounded choice</span></b></h3><p><span>The word “clarity” carries great weight. Remember that when a former resident compiled a months-long report on harm at MAPLE in 2022—commissioned by Soryu himself—his verdict, delivered in passing months later, was that it </span><i><span>lacked clarity</span></i><span>. Case closed, no discussion.</span></p><p><span>“Trustworthy” is the master currency: the more Soryu ascribed trustworthiness to someone, the more esteem, rank, and access to the coveted “solo retreats” they were given. He formalized a scoring system for it. One criterion was “do you trust your teacher?”—to which he added, “it’s okay if you don’t but you shouldn’t train with them and should leave.”</span></p><p><span>That offer—</span><i><span>it’s okay if you don’t trust me, you just shouldn’t train here</span></i><span>—is what Janja Lalich explores in </span><a href="https://www.amazon.com/Bounded-Choice-Believers-Charismatic-Cults/dp/0520240189" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><i><span>Bounded Choice</span></i></a><span>. High-control groups commonly hold an explicit “people are welcome to leave any time” stance, but the dynamics make leaving costly: loss of belonging and attachment, loss of basic needs like housing, and the risk of being labelled as lacking integrity, ungrateful, traitors, or protecting their weaknesses. The likelier outcome is to compartmentalize the distrust away.</span></p><p><span>The words that might give a person a handle on their experience are preemptively neutralized. </span><i><span>Cult</span></i><span> was a “psyop used by the global suicide cult to discredit anyone trying to live differently.” </span><i><span>Trauma</span></i><span> was a humanist way of misunderstanding pain. </span><i><span>Abuse</span></i><span> and </span><i><span>harm</span></i><span> imply a victim, and victim mentality is its own disqualifier. Using these terms in their mainstream sense lets MAPLE reflexively label you a humanist and reject your perspective.</span></p><p><span>Soryu would also often take a term into abstraction: when people said that harm had been done, he and leadership would ask "what even is harm?". Another time when people felt that femininity was being trampled in the community (empathy, creativity, affection), he and leadership would respond, “What even is ‘the feminine’?” and stall the conversation in philosophy instead of getting concrete about people’s complaints.</span></p><p><span>“Taking personal responsibility” is another standard line. We were regularly given talking-to’s just before retreats that we were only allowed in the retreat if we stated we took total personal responsibility for what happens in our own mind. It’s good to take responsibility for one’s mind, but as MAPLE employs it there are no victims and therefore no wrongdoing, regardless of impact. Soryu can treat people however he wants with impunity and retroactively deem anyone who leaves feeling harmed unfit for the training. This hyper-emphasis on personal responsibility that precludes victimhood was also a core part of the doctrine of NXIVM – another prominent modern high-control group.</span></p><h3><b><span>The Manichaean landscape</span></b></h3><p><span>A defining feature of life at MAPLE is the hyper-regular reminders of apocalyptic threat—AI, nuclear war, climate change, societal collapse, species extinction—all common threads in Soryu’s talks. I remember him describing a recent experience of his, saying “I was walking in the forest and saw a big flash briefly light up the sky on the horizon, and I thought ‘there goes New York!’ [from an atomic bomb]…” followed by something to the effect of “that’s the kind of mind I want for all of you.”</span></p><p><span>What differentiates MAPLE from many historical high-control groups is that the risks are real—not UFOs or a dated apocalypse prophecy, but genuine 21st-century threats. People do need support developing the capacity to face them, but MAPLE isn’t doing this in a healthy way: the fear of the outside world was over-amplified and leveraged coercively. The meme below, circulated by members including in a tweet by their executive director, captures the tone. MAPLE repeatedly calls mainstream society “the mainstream cult”—implying not just that MAPLE is uniquely correct, but that everyone outside it is in the actually-dangerous cult.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 58.61065016875131%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Z7pjBbK9qujhGbxws/ykwbecrj8aehnxyhea5j" /></figure><h3><b><span>Us versus them, persecution narrative</span></b></h3><p><span>Hassan writes: “Fear is used to bind the group members together… The first is the creation of an outside enemy who is persecuting you.” This narrative has existed at MAPLE for years but is now reaching a crescendo: MAPLE positions itself as the sole rescuer of civilization from a broad array of enemies—mainstream culture, the global suicide cult of Western civilization, transhumanism, online critics including many alumni, and cancel culture.</span></p><p><span>A recent visitor noted “they [the MAPLE community] seem to have developed a quite worrying level of paranoia around outsiders; the idea is that the dominant society will always try to convert or kill anyone who wants to do things differently, and any members who break with the program is figured as part of that potentially deadly threat.”</span></p><p><span>After a recent visit from a New Yorker reporter, MAPLE—expecting a “hit piece”—</span><a href="https://archive.is/XhQth" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>launched a campaign</span></a><span> to preemptively discredit him, releasing a newsletter that frames him as an evangelist from the mainstream cult, with articles, audio, and video each made by a different member casting him as a deluded enemy. They’re now bracing for more: “Reporters, podcasters, and propagandists from the mainstream are coming.” It’s a clear example of the siege mentality that the high-control literature demonstrates often occurs in such communities when public scrutiny intensifies.</span></p><h3><b><span>MAPLE as possessing the one true solution to world crisis</span></b></h3><p><span>MAPLE’s explicit goals have included becoming the next World Religion, leading an emerging "world government" with "teeth" (its own military), opening 500 monasteries within 50 years to generate a civilizational sea change, and developing an AI Superintelligence that will “</span><a href="https://archive.is/JYJwD" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>turn the world into a monastery</span></a><span>.”</span></p><p><span>I’ll just let these quotes from MAPLE’s online materials speak for themselves:</span></p><p><span>From </span><a href="https://archive.is/8eFpq" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>New Vision</span></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>“He [Soryu] said that what we are doing matters, that whether we succeed or not is real, that nobody else is doing this, that we have an opportunity to change world history, that we already have something unique in the whole world, that we can offer this unique thing to everyone and that therefore we must.”</span></p></blockquote><p><span>From a statement by Soryu in the </span><a href="https://www.monasticacademy.org/quarterly-reports/2025-summer.pdf" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Summer 2025 quarterly report</span></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>“Only organizations that (1) offer an ideology that (2) allows grieving fully enough for the (3) realization of wisdom (4) by the global mind can solve global problems. That’s less than 1% of 1% of 1% of 1% of organizations worldwide. Maybe that’s just one organization.”</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Also, Soryu once shared that he was last reincarnated in a realm that was “really, really, really, really good” but quickly chose to reincarnate as a human out of compassion. He was regularly mythologized within the community as a figure of world-historical significance.</span></p><h2><b><span>EMOTION CONTROL</span></b></h2><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Z7pjBbK9qujhGbxws/rghcxxbdqrnxmjunchxp" /><p><span>Hassan opens his section on emotional control with these two paragraphs, inserted here in full because they speak to such a prominent feature of life at MAPLE that I want it emphasized:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Guilt and fear are necessary tools to keep people under control. Guilt is probably the single most important emotional lever for producing conformity and compliance. Historical guilt (e.g., the fact that the United States dropped the atomic bomb on Hiroshima), identity guilt (e.g., a thought such as “I’m not living up to my potential”), guilt over past actions (e.g., “I cheated on a test”), and social guilt (e.g., “People are dying of starvation”) can all be exploited by destructive cult leaders. However, most cult members can’t see that guilt and fear are being used to control them. They are so conditioned always to blame themselves that they respond gratefully whenever a leader points out one of their “shortcomings.”</span></p><p><i><span>—Steve Hassan, Combatting Cult Mind Control (p. 63)</span></i></p></blockquote><h3><b><span>Intermittent reinforcement and trauma bonds</span></b></h3><p><span>A defining feature of the way that Soryu relates to many people in the community—and often to the community as a whole through lectures and dharma talks—is oscillating between praise, affection, and charismatic inspiration, and episodes of guilt, shame, belittling, mockery, aggression, or public humiliation for relatively minor infractions.</span></p><p><span>The term for this is “intermittent reinforcement,” and it is a hallmark indicator for abusive relationships and is found in nearly every high-control group. When care, approval, or belonging are given inconsistently, people work harder to regain them and scan their own behavior for what will bring the next moment of relief. Over time, this can intensify to the point of forming a “trauma bond”: an enduring emotional attachment that creates such a powerful drive to receive validation/connection that it overrides clear judgment and can become a reliable source of control over that person.</span></p><p><span>As Alexandra Stein, another prominent researcher who focuses on the role of the attachment system in cults, shares in her book </span><i><span>Terror, Love, and Brainwashing</span></i><span>: It doesn’t matter how secure a person’s attachment style is prior to entering a totalizing high-control group, those groups are able to engender a disorganized attachment style.</span></p><p><span>Soryu once told the community during a dharma talk that someone in the audience of a public talk he was giving years earlier asked him the question “What’s the most effective way to control people?” Soryu said he responded simply: “Oh, easy. Praise and blame.”</span></p><h3><b><span>Guilt and shame</span></b></h3><p><span>It’s hard to stress just how much guilt and shame are features of life at MAPLE. Each of these forms listed above—historical, identity, actions, social—are prevalent. There are many possible examples to share, but here are a few:</span></p><p><span>During a two-week retreat in 2022, COVID was moving through the community. It began with accommodations—interviews outdoors for ventilation, the sick practicing apart—but Soryu progressively mocked those who needed rest, admonished us for being deterred by “a tiny little cold,” and folded everyone back into the zendo regardless of who was sick. Interviews resumed in a 9x9 room with little ventilation while Soryu himself had COVID. Symptomatic, visibly fatigued people kept the full schedule on heavily reduced sleep. His talks ran cold with disappointment, casting our exhaustion not as illness but as a failure of commitment—our practice had gone “floppy,” and he traced the rot to the gentler programming of the two prior months he was away. Needing rest became something to be ashamed of.</span></p><p><span>One MAPLE alumni shared that the message they received from Soryu during their apprenticeship was: “You’re the worst iteration of humanity that has ever existed.
