You are this malignant cancer that needs to be destroyed and made into someone new.”</span></p><p><span>In a lecture to the community, Soryu </span><a href="https://oasis-pair-49f.notion.site/Merciless-grilling-of-assistant-audio-clip-3ab6db775b6380b2a13bf889d7e4e57a" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>recounted approvingly</span></a><span> how he had broken in his assistant: “The thing that helped the most was, for months, me just grilling him mercilessly. So a lot of it is: you have to be willing to get mercilessly grilled… So I grilled him mercilessly on: ‘At this moment, earlier today, you failed. You abdicated the responsibility that was yours. It was not mine, it was yours. And because of that, their problems have dropped upon me and the community. At that moment that you entered into </span><i><span>moha</span></i><span> … you entered into your little dream world simulation life…’”</span></p><p><span>A published MAPLE Tale titled “</span><a href="https://archive.is/zyPzf" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>You just like it when your heart hurts</span></a><span>” describes the shock and pain a resident feels when another long-time resident abruptly leaves the community, and how Soryu responded to their pain:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>The residents and Soryu gathered in the main hall to discuss a recent event in the community. A resident had recently come out of a long solo retreat in which he had not maintained his integrity and then immediately left campus with his girlfriend. Many in the community were hurt and confused, including me. I had been affected very deeply by the actions of my friend and brother on the path, but I stayed silent during the group conversation. As the discussion was breaking up and we were transitioning into the next part of the evening schedule, Soryu said to me, “Why didn’t YOU say anything?”</span></p><p><span>I said, “My heart hurts”. He said, “You just like it when your heart hurts. Don’t let his betrayal make you betray us.”</span></p></blockquote><p><span>This states that their departure was coming from a lack of integrity and blithely inconsiderate of their impact on the community, and that MAPLE had done nothing wrong. I have been in contact with the girlfriend of the departing resident this tale speaks of, and her story of what led to their departure is starkly different than is presented here.</span></p><p><span>But more relevantly, Soryu pathologizes grief about a friend leaving as a perverse desire (’you just like it’), equates the resident’s silent grief itself with ‘betrayal’ of the community, and then shames them for it. It should also be noted here that multiple academic frameworks identify the suppression of grief about departing members as a high-control indicator.</span></p><p><span>In another published MAPLE Tale, “</span><a href="https://tales.monasticacademy.org/tales/maybe-you-hate-them/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Maybe you hate them</span></a><span>,” a resident asks for advice during a Q&amp;A about how to stop obsessing over people in the person's life, people who aren't present at MAPLE. Soryu answers with a question: “Maybe you hate them... is that possible?” Soryu asserts that the obsessing itself is harmful to these people. Soryu asked them “what else in the universe is harmful” and the resident offers physical violence. Soryu then folds mind-wandering into the same category, reasoning that violence too comes from perceiving an idea of a person rather than the person: “If you practice using them as a mental slave… you will use them as a mental slave.” He then prescribes shame as the cure: “even if all you’re doing is acting as if you hate them … that might be enough for motivation. Right?” The resident reports relief: “enough shame was evoked that I could stop.”</span></p><p><span>Another example is an exhortation during the retreat of January 2022. The mood of the talk was intimate as Soryu was relaying a very emotionally charged story of a tragedy he witnessed in India in his twenties. One student let out a quiet yawn while Soryu was speaking (retreatants can often be quite exhausted on these retreats, given the intensity). In a sudden reversal of energy, Soryu shouted at the student, in a tone of disgust and condemnation: “yawning!? what have </span><i><span>you</span></i><span> done with </span><i><span>your</span></i><span> life, huh!?!”—indicating the student hadn’t done much with their life relative to Soryu, who had. The mood quickly shifted back to its previous heartfelt vibe.</span></p><p><span>Something that Hassan shares, that I know quite a lot of MAPLE alumni will resonate with: “Problems are always the fault of the member, and are due to </span><i><span>his</span></i><span> weak faith, </span><i><span>his</span></i><span> lack of understanding.” I spoke to a former member recently who said Soryu is an “aikido master” of pinning everything back on you, your mind, your faults.</span></p><h3><b><span>Manufactured breakdowns in recruitment</span></b></h3><p><span>In October 2020, when Soryu was visiting OAK (the California branch of Monastic Academy), we held a 3-day retreat exclusively for five young professionals in the Bay Area. Most of them were new to this “meditation thing,” wanted to try it out on recommendation from their friend who lived at OAK, and expressed no interest in joining the community long-term. There was no mental health screening process.</span></p><p><a href="https://oasis-pair-49f.notion.site/Manipulative-recruitment-talk-3ab6db775b6380a1a619d57a4c2067f4" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Here’s a recording and transcript</span></a><span> of the following talk he gave on that retreat. Soryu told these guests: “Someone who didn’t do nearly as much harm as we [modern people] are doing... is Hitler.” He continued to tell them they were “part of the machine that’s destroying life on earth” and that “we ourselves are doing this.” He then presented a stark binary: either one wakes up, “bursts forth as the hero that we naturally are,” and walks the glorified path of integrity that Soryu represents, or one remains in what he called their “half-hearted, pretend lives” as “pathetic slaves to a system.”</span></p><p><span>He then discredited their epistemics and worldviews. “Cognition, conceiving, is delusional. Whatever model we may hold, it isn’t true.” He generalized this into a total claim about the mind they had lived by: “this mind that we have cultivated and lived by, is not trustworthy. It is not trustworthy.”</span></p><p><span>He then shared “If there isn’t a sense of urgency in your body right now, if you’re not horrified right now at yourself, then you’re not listening to this talk. You’re not paying attention. You’re not listening and you’re not carefully investigating. You’re just sitting there. That isn’t good enough anymore... you should grow up now.”</span></p><p><span>To feel no urgency, he said, was to be a bad person: “if it isn’t a constant struggle for you, well then, you’re a bad person... You’re failing in your life.” He framed his words as care for them: “the disagreement that you and I have is how important your life is. I think your life is much more important than you do, as far as I can tell. You all seem to think your lives are worthless.” And “these immature tendencies that you have to get over eventually. Get over it. Stop lying to yourself about the most important things in life and live a life that you can be proud of rather than one that you have to regret.”</span></p><p><span>To really drive the shame home, he said “You’re part of the machine that’s destroying life on earth. You should be ashamed of that.” The prescribed response was horror at who we are: “We feel horrified, and then we grieve. We feel ashamed, then we grieve, and we feel dread. We feel dread that we may continue to live in this way, that there’s no guarantee that we’re ever going to get out. And then we will have lived a meaningless life.”</span></p><p><span>He then concluded with: “It’s hard to have confidence if no one sees you as astonishing and miraculous all the time. So I understand it, but you are here now, and you have a certain amount of love directed at you, a certain amount of belief, certainly from me.”</span></p><p><span>The next day of the retreat, which was the final day, Soryu’s talk went for 3 hours and was all praise and love.</span></p><p><span>During this retreat one of the five attendees had a destabilizing psychosis-like event that I don’t know all the details of (something about Soryu holographically visiting them as they sat under a tree); this attendee oscillated between extreme distrust of Soryu to strong support, leading them to donate a large sum of money to OAK. Another attendee later flew from California out to MAPLE in Vermont to attend a retreat.</span></p><p><span>A year later, I remember the director describing how Soryu was designing another short retreat with newcomers. While I don’t remember the exact phrasing, it made clear that stacking intensity early in the container (epistemic breakdown, shame, guilt) and then bringing in the positive and affirming energy at the end was a deliberate design choice by Soryu. This sequence isn’t a coincidence. It tracks the three-stage model Steven Hassan describes in a section of Combating Cult Mind Control titled “The three steps to gaining control of the mind”: </span><b><span>unfreezing</span></b><span>, in which a person’s existing sense of self and epistemic frameworks are broken down; </span><b><span>changing</span></b><span>, in which a new framework is pressed in under emotional pressure, especially shame and guilt; and </span><b><span>refreezing</span></b><span>, in which the new identity is stabilized through belonging. It is a pattern widely documented in high-control group recruitment.</span></p><h3><b><span>Cycles of withdrawal leading to deeper devotion</span></b></h3><p><span>Soryu would periodically threaten to leave the community, framing us as too uncommitted or too corrupted by humanist ideology to be worth his teaching. The withdrawal almost invariably produced a surge of devotion and commitment from the community. In January 2022, during a retreat that followed a critical letter being publicly released by an alum, he entered the zendo to give his daily guided meditation, but this time sat in silence with a distant look, and left. That same day, he told us, “I’m abandoning you.”</span></p><h3><b><span>Aggression</span></b></h3><p><span>There was a retreat in May of 2021 where a student lay in supine position during an exhortation. This was not allowed, but given the student had just joined the community only a couple days before the retreat began, they didn’t know this. Soryu responded by interrupting his light-hearted talk to yell at the student—by “yell” I mean louder than I’ve ever heard another human shout, with extreme aggression—saying “WHAT IS THIS!?!? GET UP!! GET UP!!” and more words that I can’t remember. It was as if a bomb had gone off unexpectedly and many in the Zendo looked frozen.</span></p><p><span>Then, as soon as the shouting subsided, Soryu rapidly reversed his tone, and started talking about how much he loves snakes in this sweetsy cutesy vibe, continuing with the talk as if the shouting never happened.</span></p><p><span>In December of 2021, following a meeting that had otherwise felt routine and non-confrontational, most participants left the room. Three people remained: Soryu, myself, and a senior resident. In that smaller, private setting, the senior resident shared with Soryu that they had been sensing “cult vibes” in the community.</span></p><p><span>I don’t recall Soryu’s exact words in response, but what stood out was a sudden and marked shift in tone and intensity. His voice rose sharply, approaching shouting, and his posture and energy moved quickly into a confrontational stance. He challenged the resident to explain what they were seeing.</span></p><p><span>Based on the resident’s body language and tone of voice, they appeared to enter a freeze response. They replied, but briefly and stutteringly. No nuanced or exploratory discussion followed, and the interaction ended shortly thereafter. All three of us then left the room.</span></p><p><span>This sudden aggression was a regular feature of MAPLE during my time there.</span></p><h3><b><span>Stoking insecurity</span></b></h3><p><span>There were multiple times where I understood the clear indication from Soryu as “you’ll be insecure forever” unless you truly practice right now. On one occasion I heard this as an exact quote. He would regularly stoke insecurity and inadequacy in members, and present himself as holding the key to resolving it.</span></p><h3><b><span>Mocking and belittling</span></b></h3><p><span>Soryu would regularly mock and belittle members of the community. I’ve heard secondhand from multiple people that since I’ve left residents are now regularly referred to as first graders, apprentices as kindergarteners, and non-residents as pre-schoolers. A clear representation of the power asymmetry between Soryu and the rest of the community.</span></p><p><span>Another example is: in the final hours of every retreat there is a period where students share their insights and experience of the retreat with the whole community. It is framed as a precious opportunity to offer your wisdom to each other and the world, and people typically share vulnerable from-the-heart material. Soryu on one occasion mocked all of the shares after one retreat by saying “Really!? I mean, </span><i><span>that</span></i><span> is your </span><i><span>wisdom</span></i><span>?? Oh god.” He would regularly oscillate between praising our shares and speaking about them as if they were sub-rate self-conscious dribble.</span></p><h3><b><span>“Hoovering” when trying to leave</span></b></h3><p><span>Hoovering names the dynamic in abusive relationships where, when the victim seeks to step away, the abuser attempts to keep them in it, for example by: immense guilt-tripping, inducing fear that they will be forever insecure, insane, or unhappy without the support that the abuser alone can give, and gaslighting them about their concerns.</span></p><p><span>An ex-resident who trained at MAPLE for ~2 years told me that when they first told Soryu they wanted to leave, his whole way of relating to them changed. They sat one more retreat before leaving, and throughout it Soryu skewered their particular insecurities and woundings, and attempted to guilt them into staying. I remember Soryu making nearly a dozen comically exaggerated faces of sadness, coupled with whimpering sad-puppy sounds, when this person was announcing their departure at post-meal announcements. This person had a strong historical pattern of self-abandonment to please others, and it struck me as emotional pressure applied at the very moment they were attempting to set a boundary and step away.</span></p><p><span>Something quite similar happened to me. After telling Soryu I was considering stepping back from the teacher–student relationship due to issues of trust, I continued training and attended three more retreats with him in the spring of 2023—my final retreats under his guidance. Across them, the way he related to me shifted markedly, in interviews and in exhortations. (Personal teachings delivered in a generalized way during exhortation give Soryu a shield of plausible deniability: anyone receiving them as personal is simply “projecting.”)</span></p><p><span>He consistently framed my consideration as an egregious moral failing—that I was careless, ungrateful, selfish, and untrustworthy for contemplating this after years of training; that I was betraying him personally; and that remaining was the responsible, sane, and compassionate choice. And I was repeatedly cautioned about the danger of entering certain spiritual territory while carrying “deep psychological wounds”, and that people who step away from his guidance at such a juncture are “dangerous.” In my final interview, without ever hearing my reasons for distrust—which were extensive, and privately corroborated by multiple leaders in the community—he told me that “the pain and distrust is not clarity.”</span></p><h3><b><span>Loyalty, dedication to the mission, not complaining</span></b></h3><blockquote><p><span>Loyalty and devotion are the most highly respected emotions of all. Members are not allowed to feel or express negative emotions, except toward outsiders. Members are taught never to feel for themselves or their own needs but always to think of the group and never to complain.</span></p><p><span>— Combatting Cult Mind Control, pg. 64</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Loyalty and devotion (and gratitude and commitment to the mission) are indeed very highly respected emotions at MAPLE. We were continually taught to put others’ needs before ourselves, relieve ourselves of our trivial self-centered concerns, and give ourselves over to the mission. Members regularly praised Soryu with a lot of tears and strong emotional energy, heralded him as an icon of trustworthiness and compassion, and idealized him in dozens of ways.</span></p><p><span>Hassan elaborates: "If a person is not behaving "enthusiastically," he may be confronted by a leader and accused of being selfish or impure, or of not trying hard enough."</span></p><p><span>It is of note that joy and enthusiasm is demanded at MAPLE. It’s even in </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/16QGAuNKt8Q7-Nc5QpowmhS31yviGlrFL/edit?usp=sharing&amp;ouid=104905952796794244352&amp;rtpof=true&amp;sd=true" rel="noreferrer"><span>the contractual agreement</span></a><span> you all sign, which begins:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>"I feel joyful, blessed, and honored to participate as a residential mindfulness trainee (“Resident”) in the modern mindfulness and leadership training program (“Program”) of the Monastic Academy for the Preservation of Life on Earth (“MAPLE”)"</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Emotional distress and debilitating breakdowns (which weren’t uncommon) were routinely framed as delusion needing purification, and as signs that the training was working; disengaging mid-collapse invariably meant protecting your suffering and letting delusion win. The possibility that Soryu and the training itself may be causing harm was invariably excluded.</span></p><p><span>As for never complaining: in Spring of 2023, Soryu made sweeping changes to the monastery curriculum and to the mission and strategy of the organization. MAPLE would now have a much narrower focus on artificial intelligence and the creation of a superintelligent AI that will “turn the world into a monastery” and liberate all beings. At the same time, “increased surveillance for your own benefit” was announced. These decisions were made suddenly and unilaterally. A number of residents and people in leadership had issues with them, raising concerns about the way these changes were being made and about the ethics of surveillance and of attempting to “turn the world into a monastery.”</span></p><p><span>It was clear that Soryu did not respect those concerns. One way he and leadership responded was an evening activity, of 2 hours in length. All residents were asked to stay in silence, meander around the property or sit, and “feel our feelings” about this unilateral pivot. There was no room for discussion or input.</span></p><p><span>Here’s how Soryu </span><a href="https://oasis-pair-49f.notion.site/Flip-the-burgers-or-you-quit-quote-3ab6db775b6380bb9b88ea6b3878a2cb?source=copy_link" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>addressed these concerns</span></a><span> in a talk to the community:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>“You’re not in a position to contribute. You’re in a position to execute. When you’re on the board, we want to hear your ideas. Until then, we don’t. That’s the deal.</span></p><p><span>You don’t go to McDonald’s and say, “Should we be a restaurant? I don’t agree that a restaurant is really of maximum benefit to the world.” You don’t even say, “Should we get our beef from Brazil?” You don’t even ask that. You flip the burgers or you quit. That’s your option right there. And it’s the same here.”</span></p></blockquote><h3><b><span>Instilling a sense of indebtedness</span></b></h3><p><span>“Be grateful and give back to the community for all you’ve received” is an explicit message within MAPLE. But the way it is approached at MAPLE this gratitude becomes infused with a sense of shame-based indebtedness. For all you’ve been given, you owe your labor and loyalty.</span></p><p><span>For example, I clearly recall Soryu stating in a talk in the February 2023 retreat: “you could train in a place like MAPLE for several years, receive a new way of seeing the world, and the</span><i><span> very least</span></i><span> you owe is to spend the rest of your life ensuring that lineage thrives.”</span></p><p><span>A summarized message I heard on multiple occasions: You are given food and shelter and training, so put out your alms bowl like a good monk and help with fundraising.</span></p><p><span>When a number of people were considering leaving with concerns, Soryu—in a rather condemnatory tone while speaking to the whole community—framed this as “some people are just willing to take more than they give.”</span></p><h2><b><span>An example that fits each of the four BITE quadrants</span></b></h2><p><span>Here’s something a current MAPLE resident recently </span><a href="https://www.reddit.com/r/sorceryofthespectacle/comments/1rnm6om/system_lost_credibility_join_our_cult/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>posted onto Reddit</span></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>The system has clearly lost all credibility. You are invited to make a radical decision: totally leave the system.</span><br /><br /><span>Move to Northern Vermont. Enter hardcore spiritual training. Overturn everything you know. Get initiated. Build a new world.</span><br /><br /><span>Any alternative to the system will be labeled a “cult”. This word is a psyop to discredit all alternatives, especially if they are influenced by Asian culture.</span><br /><br /><span>Join an actual culture.</span><br /><span>Convert to a correct religion.</span><br /><span>Solve the planetary crisis.</span><br /><a href="http://monasticacademy.org/train" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>http://monasticacademy.org/train</span></a></p></blockquote><p><span>The reader is urged to “totally leave the system… enter hardcore spiritual training… get initiated” (Behavior). The Emotion lever is urgency with “solve the planetary crisis.” Sharpest is thought: “overturn everything you know” and “convert to a correct religion.” And Information: “Any alternative to the system will be labeled a ‘cult’. This word is a psyop… especially if they are influenced by Asian culture”—a textbook thought-terminating cliché that inoculates against every future critic using this term.</span></p><p><span>I’ve seen this with another social media post another member made recently, where a departing trainee “who failed out of the training” and had concerns about non-accountability of authoritarian leadership was being framed as “xenophobic.” Screenshot:</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 56.042657764450354%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Z7pjBbK9qujhGbxws/snzuppwxxidnrbxrj4ak" /></figure><h2><b><span>Justifications for severe harm &amp; defense of omnicide</span></b></h2><p><span>Robert Lifton, another prominent cult researcher, names </span><i><span>dispensing of existence</span></i><span> (the leader’s assumed right to decide whose life and wellbeing matter) as common to every totalist system he studied.</span></p><p><span>Soryu maintains that killing every human on the planet to protect the rest of life is ethically defensible. He shared on numerous occasions that this was his explicit life purpose from roughly ages 9 to 19. I once heard him claim he saw CRISPR (a gene modifying technique that could be used to create a novel bio-weapon) as his best available means. Here’s the </span><a href="https://oasis-pair-49f.notion.site/A-retreat-talk-with-a-high-density-of-red-flags-3ab6db775b638067927bcd464a56a13f" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>audio and transcript</span></a><span> of a talk where he recounted telling people this as a young man, “…they would recoil, because they could see in my eyes that I meant it: I want to kill you, and your family, and they would become afraid…”, which led him to realize that “…no human being can be trusted, they’re all against me, they’re all against the benefit of all beings, their goal is the destruction of all that is beautiful … these selfish beings cannot be trusted.”</span></p><p><span>Soryu has also held that traumatizing his students, “even to the point of being incapable of participating in the economy for the rest of their lives,” was justified by the urgency of the mission, and once asked a roomful of us to settle what proportion of traumatized students would be acceptable: “is it 20%? 50%? 100%!?!”</span></p><p><span>This dispensing of existence also reached both the neighboring town and the MAPLE community itself. I was tasked by the director with transcribing a recording of a leadership meeting discussing how MAPLE should weather near-term societal collapse (I was told it was too sensitive for other students to hear; I unfortunately no longer have access to this recording). MAPLE sits in a Vermont town of roughly 700 people, many of them low-income, and its campus has assets that would be valuable in a collapse scenario, like solar power, large buildings, a garden, and a tractor. Asked how many townspeople it would take in, Soryu closed the question off, aghast—“take people in?! Oh god...”—setting the number at zero. Presumably backing out of mutual aid during disaster can be justified because MAPLE is the torch-bearer of the new trustworthy civilization that will emerge post-collapse (he explicitly framed MAPLE this way in a </span><a href="https://podcasts.apple.com/us/podcast/soryu-forall-responding-to-collapse-virtue-and/id1057220344" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>podcast interview</span></a><span>) and needs protection.</span></p><p><span>When someone in leadership noted that survival would be harder for MAPLE than for ordinary bunker-builders because we would have the added task of maintaining communal harmony together, Soryu asked, in a tone indicating disagreement, “we will?” The person responded “...what, you mean we set up an authoritarian structure instead?” Soryu said “Yes.” Then: “if half the [MAPLE] community starts running around like chickens with their heads cut off and die within the first week, just let that process play out.”</span></p><h3><b><span>Where is MAPLE at now?</span></b></h3><p><span>MAPLE hasn’t always fit the “cult” caricature to the degree they do now. High-control groups typically follow a developmental arc. I joined when there was still burgeoning youthful zeal and innovation. They now seem to be clearly in the stage where—after high profile departures and online criticism—the control dynamics have reached a crescendo and they are now acting more transparently “culty.”</span></p><p><span>They are struggling to recruit new members at a faster rate than they lose them. There are currently only roughly 10 people living there full time, down from ~35 at its apex. They currently seem to be engaged in a coordinated recruitment campaign where the majority of residents are using their social media accounts to promote their upcoming residency and to debase critics.</span></p><p><span>As Jamie Wheal notes in his </span><a href="https://cdn.prod.website-files.com/5e74e8349194e2f76b9ffcd1/5e754938d25345a72291f0a3_Ethical%20Cult%20Checklist.pdf" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>ethical cult checklist</span></a><span>: “Many cult-like communities will initially present with palpable energy, enthusiasm, celebration, joy, and growth, precisely because they are harnessing many of the techniques described above [in his checklist]. This can create cognitive dissonance for someone applying the “by their fruits ye shall know them” filter—while the fruits appear to be abundant. Using charismatic transmission, encouraging regressive catharsis, controlling ecstatic rituals of bonding, and advocating for magical thinking consistently produce potent effects quickly. It is not that they don’t work, it’s that they work only too well (until they inevitably don’t).”</span></p><p><span>It’s clear to me that MAPLE is in the “until they inevitably don’t” stage.</span></p><h2><b><span>Concluding the BITE analysis</span></b></h2><p><span>I’ve walked through the BITE framework at such length because the case for “MAPLE is a high-control group” is not made through any single anecdote. Taken as a whole, a new, more concerning picture emerges: a community with starkly centralized power, mutually reinforcing means of control over members’ minds and lives, self-sealing feedback structures, and an alarming number of members leaving with devastating degrees of harm, some of which I pointed to at the open of the letter: suicidality, psychosis, PTSD, panic attacks, and loss of agency.</span></p><h3><b><span>My complicity</span></b></h3><p><span>In December 2021, Shekinah Alegra—a former apprentice of OAK, the California branch of Monastic Academy—published a public letter detailing a sexual assault by OAK’s Executive Director. She described an institutional response that excluded her from meetings about the incident and pressured her to sign a letter characterizing the encounter as consensual, and laid out concerns about misleading recruitment, inadequately trained and inexperienced leadership, abuses of power, financial practices, unsafe retreat practices, and retraumatizing care. She situated her account within roughly twenty to twenty-five other reports of negligence or abuse known to her at the time, and a number of MAPLE alumni commented in support.</span></p><p><span>The sexual assault allegation was engaged with internally as a matter of individual misconduct by the former OAK Executive Director (by then already demoted), where he was interrogated by the whole community. Every other concern the letter raised was largely dismissed, and the community framed the people who supported Shekinah’s letter as alumni who were simply unfit for Soryu’s “aggressive teaching style.” This reframe had little to do with the letter itself: Shekinah never trained under Soryu, and her critique was not that the training was too intense but everything laid out above.</span></p><p><span>In the wake of the letter, there was a rallying cry within the community to defend Soryu. Around then he told us (paraphrased from memory): “People say favorable things about their spiritual teachers all the time, but sometimes backtrack it later. One way to demonstrate you actually stand behind your teacher is to say so publicly.” And in the first retreat after the letter, in January 2022, Soryu threatened to leave the community if we were incapable of receiving his true teachings—engendering a lot of zeal to demonstrate we were worthy, unlike those expressing concerns online.</span></p><p><span>Within hours of completing that retreat, I sent an email to MAPLE’s board defending Soryu using the simplified “aggressive teaching” frame. An excerpt (</span><a href="https://oasis-pair-49f.notion.site/2022-letter-to-the-board-36c6db775b63806ea075e7a98527cc0e" rel="noreferrer"><span>full email here</span></a><span>):</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Soryu’s teaching style, which many have called aggressive, some have called arrogant, few have even called totalitarian, has also been described to me by the many who’ve come through this training in the last year and a half as jaw-droppingly on-point, deeply compassionate and kind, wise and patient and tough-loving and clearly motivated by the desire to bring our lives into true accord with the Dharma and enable us to be the stewards of change we want - and the worlds needs - us to be. […] It would be a great loss if other dedicated practitioners didn’t have the opportunity to experience this style of training in contrast with the gentler style so abundant in the US.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>I deeply regret this and take full ownership of its consequences. Whether or not my email made any difference to the board’s inaction, I played a role in upholding the structures that later harmed my friends in the community. To all of them I apologize.</span></p><p><span>While none of this lessens what I did, the email was also the system functioning as designed. It was structurally incentivized for me and many other members to defend the community using these same framings at that time. Me writing an unsolicited defense of an organization accused of coverup, using this simplified frame, was itself part of what Shekinah’s letter was describing.</span></p><p><span>The January 2022 email wasn’t the first time I had played a role in protecting the organization from the consequences of what happened to Shekinah. When everything unfolded at OAK in the winter of 2020, I flew from Vermont as part of a small team to manage the situation.</span></p><p><span>As far as I am aware, the account circulating in the broader community at the time was that Shekinah and the ED had entered a mutual romantic connection that violated monastic boundaries, and that the sexual encounter between them had been consensual. That was the account I received and held. It was not her own: the romantic connection was real and mutual, but she knew even in those weeks that something was wrong with what had happened to her—and, as is common in the aftermath of sexual assault, fully naming it took time and considerable work. Her December 2021 letter describes her raising concerns about consent and a coverup in a May 2021 email to MAPLE leadership—six months after the incident, seven months before her letter. To my knowledge nothing of that escalation reached the broader community until the public letter, which is where the naming of the encounter as assault first reached members like me.</span></p><p><span>I’m generally considered quite affable, so one of my central tasks at OAK was to keep Shekinah as settled as possible up until her month-long apprenticeship was over. The frame I had received was that she was unstable, and that her departure was something we needed to manage with care. I held that frame without question. In hindsight, the frame was doing quiet work: it converted her distress, her dissent, and her open anger at how things were being handled—much of it an understandable response to what was happening around her—into symptoms to be managed. And it exempted everyone else from the same scrutiny. Nobody at OAK was stable in those weeks, least of all the leadership managing the situation. There’s a lot that could be said about this time, but the primary thing I want to focus on is the most serious harm I caused while at MAPLE/OAK.</span></p><p><span>In her final days at OAK, two others and I were tasked with drafting what was essentially a “Don’t Sue Us” document for her to sign—a five-page waiver borrowed from somewhere on the internet (this was separate from the consent document she and the ED were pressured to sign, before I arrived at OAK). Given my positive standing with Shekinah, getting her to sign it fell to me, and we strategized how to get her to sign something she likely otherwise wouldn’t—all under the guidance of OAK’s leadership, including Soryu by phone from Vermont. The plan was that I’d ask her in a moment when she was in a good mood. I carried the document in my back pocket for two days until the right moment came:</span></p><p><span>On her final night, driving her to the train, I took her and the ED—still quite romantically interested in each other—up to a beautiful vista overlooking the entire Bay Area, and gave them privacy while I waited in the car. After about 20 minutes in this serene and romantic setting, and just a few minutes before we had to leave for the train, I left the car and walked up to them with the document. In a playful tone, I invited her to sign this document, which would break the serenity of the moment—and make us late for the train—for her to actually read all five pages of it. I called it a “Don’t Sue Us document”. She chuckled and quipped “Y’all need to get your shit together” and signed it without reading. I had spent 10 days building her trust and capitalized on it; an hour later she left on the train, and it was the last time I spoke with her until we crossed paths at a potluck years later.</span></p><p><span>A few weeks after her departure, when the document was in my backpack in the back of my car that was parked in an SF parking lot, my window was smashed and my backpack stolen. The document—which we had no copies of—as well as my computer and passport, were gone forever.</span></p><p><span>Even within the frame I was operating in at the time, what I did was harmful. The frame was both that the encounter had been consensual, and that Shekinah was unstable and needed managing—rather than a person responding, at least in part, to what the organization had done and was doing. Within my frame at the time, the waiver felt like a reasonable precaution and the strategizing felt skillful. I did not experience any of this as causing harm in the doing of it. I experienced it as competent execution of a task I had been given. Yet the organizational response to a “consensual affair that violated the container” should not look like exclusion from meetings, pressured letters, and strategized waiver extractions from someone framed as unstable. Someone with intact moral perception would have seen that. I’m accountable for not seeing it, regardless of what I knew or didn’t know at the time.</span></p><p><span>Shekinah and I have completed a private mediated process of repair, which I’m grateful for her participation in. It was only this past June, shortly before our final mediated meeting, that I read the post she published on the LessWrong forum—</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zbQapwn2hGNozcGsD/why-i-define-my-experience-at-the-monastic-academy-as-sexual" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Why I Define My Experience At the Monastic Academy As Sexual Assault</span></a><span>—in which she describes in detail what happened between her and OAK’s Executive Director. What she describes is unambiguously sexual assault, and I believe her.</span></p><p><span>She has also reviewed this section, and I’m grateful for the clarity she brought to it.</span></p><p><span>Last thing I’ll say here is that I can recall a number of times people came to me, particularly new people, with the question “Can I trust this place? Should I trust Soryu?” and my answer was “yes.” I would present MAPLE as an edgy but still ethical variety of spiritual community. Given my current understanding—that MAPLE is indeed a high-control group with many problematic dynamics and severe harms it has avoided accountability for—I no longer believe it is in anyone’s better interest to join MAPLE, and I deeply regret and apologize to them for saying otherwise.</span></p><h3><b><span>A koan I am still sitting with</span></b></h3><p><span>What follows might sound like a return to the kind of defense I just confessed to writing in the email above. But in 2022 I defended Soryu in lieu of engaging with the concerns being raised; what follows is not in lieu of anything in this letter, but sits alongside it all.</span></p><p><span>Since leaving there has been a creative tension between my gratitude for what I’ve received from Soryu and my alarm at his behavior. Holding both of these simultaneously has been a very valuable koan for me. He has, in fact, helped many people throughout their lives release neuroses and addictions and realize the life-giving profundities of the Buddha Dharma. This is not to be discounted.</span></p><p><span>The Soryu who positively shaped my life and practice in ways I continue to greatly benefit from, and the Soryu whose conduct I have documented above are the same person. The community that gave me some of the deepest friendships I have ever known is the same community where the dynamics in this letter played out. That a place and a person can be both of these things at once can be a challenging thing to hold, especially for those who never experienced MAPLE firsthand. Too often people want the story where it was all bad. Also, seemingly, current members want the story where MAPLE is a shining beacon of truth and goodness, and that all former members with concerns are deluded. Neither is true.</span></p><p><span>While I caution against any move to collapse the complexity of Soryu’s personhood into a dehumanized “villain” caricature, I do still firmly believe there needs to be ongoing scrutiny of his behavior, and the patterns of harm need to be brought to an end. Hence:</span></p><h3><b><span>Why I’m speaking publicly</span></b></h3><p><span>As shared earlier, there have been multiple major, and many smaller, attempts to bring feedback to the organization throughout its history, each to little avail. To quickly recap: In 2023 a group of former members compiled a ~50 page report—including testimonials from two of MAPLE’s cofounders—overviewing the problematic dynamics at MAPLE, and then sent it to MAPLE’s </span><a href="https://archive.is/YuNQu" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>board</span></a><span>. This only led to two members resigning from the board (of which Soryu is the chairman) and then being replaced by two of Soryu’s more loyal former students. In January 2022, at a time when the organization was receiving heavy public scrutiny due to Shekinah’s open letter, Soryu sanctioned an internal review on harm to students. It was compiled and then submitted to Soryu, only to be dismissed by him as “lacking clarity.” There were also multiple such attempts that occurred prior to my joining in 2020.</span></p><p><span>I’ve personally attempted to provide my concerns directly to MAPLE’s leadership, only to be received with either preemptive ad-hominem dismissal or stonewalling. Multiple alumni were shocked that I was brave enough to even attempt such a thing.</span></p><p><span>Beyond that, former members with concerns are regularly dismissed as lacking integrity, being insufficiently committed to the mission, being deeply deluded and projecting, or otherwise betraying the sangha.</span></p><p><span>Despite all of this, MAPLE still publicly claims that they are open to receiving feedback. MAPLE encourages people to keep all feedback “in relationship,” and speaking concerns publicly is framed as an egregious betrayal. This is the structure Janja Lalich—the high-control researcher that developed the Bounded Choice model—describes as a self-sealing system: making direct internal feedback the only ethically sanctioned path, then ensuring that path is consistently unproductive and costly by responding with shaming, gaslighting, minimizing, slander, and denial.</span></p><p><span>The high-control literature shows that the most reliable lever to produce positive reform in such groups is sustained public scrutiny. Even then, adequate reform is rare. The more common response is doubling down.</span></p><p><span>In the years since leaving I have been asking these questions, feeling compelled to do something, but without knowing: </span><i><span>What is integrity here? How do I reconcile what I’ve received with what I’ve witnessed?</span></i></p><p><span>For years, my answer was to attempt to help friends who’ve left to reclaim their sovereignty and heal, to hold space for friends who were still there to progressively discern for themselves it was the right decision to leave, and encourage people not to go there. I continue to learn new stories of abuse and debilitation, and I know that I’ve heard only a narrow number of the total. Some of them are more egregious than anything written in this letter, yet are either too unconfirmed to be included or I have been explicitly asked not to include.</span></p><p><span>When I’ve shared the perspective laid out in this letter privately with other alumni, I’ve seen the relief it brings. It offers new coherence to their understanding of what happened at MAPLE and lends sanity to the doubts and concerns that they have been told were illegitimate and delusional. I’m now sharing my perspective publicly so it can reach all who might benefit from hearing it.</span></p><p><span>I also hope that this letter will add to the </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5-rnxZHtjwM&amp;t=1s" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><span>growing</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-196078795" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><span>chorus</span></a><span> of </span><a href="https://medium.com/@monasticacademyresident/my-experience-inside-soryu-foralls-ai-centered-monastic-academy-769deefe6e34" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>alumni</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://medium.com/@shekinahalegra/dear-monastic-academy-and-community-members-49c25d9646a4" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>speaking</span></a><span> their concerns publicly, and help make it less emotionally, relationally, and reputationally costly for other former members to speak their concerns, should they choose. Another hope is that this letter will help create the conditions for the harm to cease, and for what was truly beautiful about MAPLE to continue to flourish—likely in new forms—unburdened by the past. Finally, I hope that this message can reach the people considering applying there. I continue to see young people join MAPLE either with no understanding of the history of pain and broken-hearted departures, or having only heard MAPLE’s ungenerous and deceptive narratives about them.</span></p><h3><b><span>Final words</span></b></h3><p><span>MAPLE’s explicit vision was beautiful—preservation of life on earth, a society rooted in wisdom and love, flourishing for all beings—and I want to protect that mission, and the means of achieving it, from the kinds of problematic behavior documented above. I continue to believe that the world desperately needs communities of practice showing up to its many crises wholeheartedly. I have zero doubts about the profundity of the dharma and its capacity to gently guide people and society towards wisdom and love. I continue to advocate for “developmental containers” that broadly cultivate spiritual, psychological, and relational capacities as a necessary ingredient in resolving society’s biggest challenges.</span></p><p><span>There’s a way a person is shaped when moving like a flock with a crew of peers: sleeping, chanting, meditating, exercising, working, eating, and studying all at the same precise time, to the same tune, day in, day out. There’s a self-transcendence and merging with a collective that is deeply nourishing to the human psyche. It was for this reason and many more that leaving was painful. When I was leaving, some of my last remarks to the community during my going-away ceremony were “I’m going, but I’m still here.” I have at times tried to shake myself of this commitment but found myself inexplicably yoked. This sangha remains sacred to me.</span></p><p><span>I’m concerned about the wellbeing of the people currently living there. I honor their sovereignty and they can choose as they wish, but I also encourage them to take some time away from MAPLE to consider what is in this letter. I also encourage their loved ones to support them in an ongoing process of inquiry, and—should they choose to leave—with integration and healing.</span></p><p><span>Please be discerning when speaking about this with people who have historically been involved with MAPLE. It can be a sensitive, emotionally charged, and disorienting topic, and everyone has their own relationship with MAPLE and their own understanding of what happened there. Please check in before bringing the topic up to see if they are willing to discuss this with you.</span></p><p><span>If you’re my friend and you’ve been impacted in any way—positively, negatively, or both—by this letter, please reach out to me. I’ll respond as my capacities allow.</span></p><p><span>While I don’t know what will happen following the release of my letter—fate will unfold as it will—allow me to speak a little to my own personal hopes: I hope we can find ways to fulfill MAPLE’s vision of the preservation of life on Earth in a way that is truly beautiful, and does not necessitate coercion. I pray Soryu accepts and receives the support he needs to address whatever it is that has him acting in these ways. I pray that all who’ve been adversely impacted can find renewed resilience and an enduring self-trust, and that all voices can be heard without fear of invalidation or retaliation. I pray for everyone in this sangha and all beings beyond to be safe, happy, and free.</span></p><p><span>With care,</span><br /><span>Sky</span></p><hr /><p><i><span>Writing this letter took months of full-time work, and I’m grateful to the few friends who covered my basic life expenses so that I could do it: Ben Pace, Oliver Habryka, Will Petillo, and an anonymous supporter. I am also grateful for smaller donations from people who cared about information being shared (who did not engage with the content substantially), from Eli Tyre and an anonymous donor. These people did not guide my conclusions about MAPLE; my perspective was formed long before any support arrived.</span></i></p><p><i><span>For valuable comments on drafts, my appreciation to Justis Mills, Ozy Brennan, Aella, Matthew ‘Vaniver’ Gray, Rob Bensinger, Tsvi Benson-Tilsen, Georgia Ray, Damon Sasi, and Shekinah Alegra. These people helped improve this letter in many ways, any remaining mistakes are mine. I also have deep appreciation for the many friends who supported me in innumerable ways over the years, at MAPLE and following leaving.</span></i></p><p><i><span>Finally, my deepest gratitude to Ben Pace. This letter would not exist without him: he read draft after draft over many months, his questions made it clearer and more honest, and his support never wavered. Thank you, Ben.</span></i></p><hr /><h1><span>Post Script</span></h1><h3><b><span>Testimony gathering</span></b></h3><p><span>If you are a member of the sangha who would like to have a story or essay of yours included on this Substack, please fill out </span><a href="https://forms.gle/Ak9hgs2Zk22VzJAu7" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>the Support Form</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>Posts made to this Substack will be curated. Not for alignment with any particular view, but for some combination of perceived effort, value-add, and good-faith intent. Anonymous posts are welcome.</span></p><p><span>I am also attempting to maintain a </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/1TZX19G5y4MTOctjSAULVzKbqbpIOqGuv_zMmFkTlADY/edit?usp=sharing" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>comprehensive page</span></a><span> of testimonials and MAPLE-relevant content. Please fill out </span><a href="https://forms.gle/Pu9MBT4j9ERDS25Q9" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>the Materials form</span></a><span> if you have something you’d like to have added to this list. The large majority of submissions will be welcomed, excluding submissions obviously spammy or overtly bad-faith.</span></p><h3><b><span>Stay informed</span></b></h3><p><span>To receive further testimonials, follow-up essays, and more MAPLE-related discussion—from members and friends of the sangha—subscribe to this Substack. It will always be free and never accepting of paid subscriptions. It is currently managed by me, though ownership may transfer or expand with time.</span></p><p><span>There is also a bank of audio recordings of Soryu, online materials written by MAPLE, and analyses of each, that will be released in chunks throughout the coming weeks. There will also be deeper explorations of the dynamics briefly introduced in this letter.</span></p><h3><b><span>Recovery funds</span></b></h3><p><span>There are many people who’ve been substantially negatively impacted by their time at MAPLE: needing to see and pay for a therapist for months or years, struggled to hold a steady job, hospital bills (for example from panic attacks), and more. I have known many people that took over a year to get back on track, and some people for whom this is still an ongoing source of significant distress.</span></p><p><span>There are many people who could use some support in their post-MAPLE journey. Some have historically found attending retreats in other communities as helpful, others were benefited through therapy, others through circling retreats. For everyone it’s different. Some are actively struggling, some have just become more stable, while many more have been stable for some time. I am interested in fundraising money to send to these people. If you may be interested in donating thousands of dollars, I have a non-profit who is willing to accept these funds, and I could disperse them later.</span></p><p><span>If you’d like to support the people impacted by MAPLE, here’s one concrete way. If you are potentially interested in donating thousands of dollars for this purpose, please reach out to me and let me know via </span><a href="https://forms.gle/Ak9hgs2Zk22VzJAu7" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>the Support form</span></a><span> and I will get back to you. I will write a follow-up post if I get serious interest, with a more concrete project proposal.</span></p><h3><b><span>To Soryu and MAPLE</span></b></h3><p><span>Lastly, I’d like to extend a personal invitation to Soryu and MAPLE leadership to write a formal response to this letter. This extends to the board, which (along with Soryu) includes </span><a href="https://www.zakstein.org/about.html" rel="noreferrer"><span>Zak Stein</span></a><span>, the Harvard-trained philosopher of education and cofounder of the </span><a href="https://consilienceproject.org/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Consilience Project</span></a><span> and the</span><a href="https://worldphilosophyandreligion.org/" rel="noreferrer"><span> Center for World Philosophy and Religion</span></a><span>, and Maitrī Huffaker. Maitrī has lived at MAPLE for ~5 years, much of which was spent training under Soryu. Her husband, Ānandabodhi Becker, is the executive director of MAPLE.</span></p><p><span>Their response (should they choose to write one), will be posted on this Substack as soon as I receive it, without any framing by me. I’ll write a followup response sometime thereafter. Scrutiny is welcome from anyone, not just MAPLE.</span></p><p><span>Writing something that requires specificity, evidence, restraint, and privacy protection has been a significant undertaking, and I have no doubt done it imperfectly. I welcome feedback on where I have fallen short. For all inquiries and feedback, I can be reached privately at </span><a href="mailto:kylehubbard962@gmail.com"><span>kylehubbard962@gmail.com</span></a><span> or via the </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/forms/d/e/1FAIpQLSe4R3aIhmCZiE2bl2GWb3sdGs-IVVmSsOw72G-FmPNSdN1VjQ/viewform" rel="noreferrer"><span>Support Form</span></a><span>.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnvg79u32gymn"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefvg79u32gymn">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Throughout this document I use the term </span><i><span>high-control group</span></i><span>. While often used interchangeably with “cult,” I use that word sparingly because of its caricatured and frequently ungenerous deployment in mainstream discourse—too often weaponized in ways that dehumanize leaders, victimize members, and dismiss both the genuine merit of a group’s guiding ideology and the real benefit people receive from training there. It’s also worth noting that MAPLE at times self-describes as a cult, and has declared publicly that the world needs more cults—equating them with “ideological startups.”</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfjo75io14rs"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffjo75io14rs">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It will also be relevant for anyone invested in the wellbeing of the movements MAPLE is loosely affiliated with: American Buddhism, Metamodernism, Effective Altruism, and the Bay Area rationalist community. And, of course, anyone at all, as high-control dynamics exist on a spectrum and can be found to various degrees in all human communities.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnctdzhbadc7g"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefctdzhbadc7g">^</a></strong></sup></span><div href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref284jpyzfmro">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This is longer than my time there, because some of the cases happened after I had left.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fntvg0tp6zkep"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreftvg0tp6zkep">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Here’s a longer, though still not comprehensive, list of other impacts that many have experienced:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Panic attacks and spells of dissociation</span></li><li value="2"><span>Major contexts—to articulate concerns about MAPLE without fear of reprisal or without speaking within MAPLE’s restrictive frames</span></li><li value="5"><span>Getting enmeshed in other coercive environments due to conditioning developed/deepened at MAPLE</span></li><li value="6"><span>Falling into shame spirals that last hours or days</span></li><li value="7"><span>Feelings of being “defiled” or “ruined”</span></li><li value="8"><span>An inability to trust others and cultivate intimacy</span></li><li value="9"><span>Falling into shame spirals that last hours or occurs on a spectrum, and everyone engages in it to some degree. I am not using it to imply conscious intent to deceive. My aim throughout this document is not to psychoanalyze anyone at MAPLE. My focus is on observable behaviors, dynamics, and their impact.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fneamibaa8nqa"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefeamibaa8nqa">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Only one such story is public, you can listen to his 4-hr testimony </span><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-196078795" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>here</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjiyq2623mu9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjiyq2623mu9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>In my time there, I was under the impression that the executive director and groundskeeper were eventually given a small salary, and the Head Teacher—most of the time Soryu—was earning a salary of $30k throughout. I expect the Head Teacher received the highest salary, but to be clear I was never shown the internal accounting, and could be mistaken about these things.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnw1ya6l132mi"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefw1ya6l132mi">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I should note that I don't believe MAPLE tells its members not to talk to ex-members; instead it falsely destroys their reputations and turns them into cautionary tales. It is common for critical ex-members to be shunned, and for current members to not speak to them again the moment they leave the community.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnidbbkid4b3"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefidbbkid4b3">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It is at the timestamp 3:32:57 from </span><a href="https://youtu.be/1XH4WLz2peY?si=Qa-Kk4NC_MfKs_js" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><span>this video</span></a><span> uploaded by MAPLE 2 months ago.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9loqccdotaw"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9loqccdotaw">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I wrote about ideological indoctrination in the information control section above because it was necessary context to understand the rest of that section, though sticking firmly to the framework would’ve had me include it here.&nbsp;</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7i3rgbqdwzh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7i3rgbqdwzh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Quoting Hassan:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Perhaps the most widely used and effective way to control cult member's thoughts is thought-stopping rituals. Members are taught to use thought-stopping on themselves. They are told it will help them grow or be more effective. Whenever a cult member begins to experience a "bad" thought, he uses thought­-stopping to drown out the "negativity" and center himself, thus learning how to shut out anything that threatens his reality.</span></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><br /></p><p><span>Different groups use different thought­-stopping techniques: concentrated praying, chanting aloud or silently, meditating, "speaking in tongues," singing, or humming. These actions, many of them ordinarily useful and valuable, are perverted in destructive cults. They become quite mechanical because the person is programmed to activate them at the first sign of doubt, anxiety, or uncertainty. In a matter of weeks the technique becomes ingrained. It becomes so automatic, in fact, that the person is usually not even aware that he had just had a "bad" thought. He is only aware that he is chanting or ritualizing all of a sudden. By using thought­-stopping, members think they are growing when in reality they are just making themselves into addicts. After leaving a cult that employs extensive thought­-stopping techniques, a person goes through a difficult withdrawal process before he can overcome the addiction.</span></p></blockquote></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Z7pjBbK9qujhGbxws/the-high-control-dynamics-at-maple-1#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Intellectual Property](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/87SzarZDjywv6nwSd/intellectual-property)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Last weekend I made myself a website. I had wanted to for ages—getting clients through word of mouth alone wasn’t cutting it. But only now, with Claude Code, was it easy enough.</span></p><p><span>I was glad the marketing was paying off when I received an email from a potential client. It wasn’t the sort of client I was expecting. I was hoping to catch a few more cheating husbands and delinquent teenagers.</span></p><p><span>Simpson, a young tech founder, was casually dressed in a t-shirt and jeans when he stepped into my office.</span></p><p><span>“Our competitor—they seem to be selling the same RL environments to similar clients. Let’s say we build something and sell to lab X. Before we even get on a call with lab Y, we hear that they’ve already purchased the same envs from Purge. It’s very suspicious. Purge has barely any employees—they call themselves a superlean startup. But they are matching our output and quality. How?”</span></p><p><span>“Maybe they are just good?”</span></p><p><span>Simpson looked at me dismissively, as if I’d suggested his mother was moonlighting slinging envs.</span></p><p><span>“No, it can’t be. Their envs are too similar. We recently converted a failing stable into a testing rig for horse-grooming robots. Guess who bought twenty horses in the town down the road?”</span></p><p><span>“And so you think they have a man on the inside?”</span></p><p><span>“Precisely! But I can’t figure out who. We have thirty employees, all of them seem to be fine upstanding folk. That’s where I need your help.”</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span>I turned up to the Programize office at 9am on Wednesday. Employees were sitting at their desks and working quietly. Most of them had a Claude Code window open. Only Simpson had a private office. I walked in and greeted him.</span></p><p><span>“Thank you so much for coming. Is there anything you need from me or will you just get started?”</span></p><p><span>“I suppose I need a pretext under which to speak to all your employees. Perhaps I can say that I’m considering taking a job here and wanted to find out what it’s like to work at Programize.”</span></p><p><span>“You’re a prospective Programoid? Sounds good to me!”</span></p><p><span>I walked out of Simpson’s office and approached the engineer sitting outside.</span></p><p><span>“Hey, nice to meet you! I’m thinking of working with you guys but wanted to swing by the place first. What are you working on right now?”</span></p><p><span>“I’m building a dog tastebud simulator. It’ll serve as one of the reward terms in our dog food formulation env.”</span></p><p><span>“Is all your work animal-themed? Simpson told me about the horse-grooming robots…”</span></p><p><span>“Oh absolutely not. Go speak to Kyle over there—he works on chess.”</span></p><p><span>I approached Kyle, noticing a stack of chess boards and boxes of unusual chess pieces underneath his desk.</span></p><p><span>“Hey Kyle! I heard you work on chess? Would be curious to learn more about what you’re building. I’m checking out the office today, thinking to join you Programoids.”</span></p><p><span>“Ah yes… chess. Well actually I am building chess pieces, in CAD software. Have you seen those novelty chess sets? There are all these cool designs. I build mockups of them for AI models to recreate. But I’ll be honest, the main reason I chose this project is because I love 3D-printing custom chess sets. I’ll probably be fired soon anyway.”</span></p><p><span>This was my first lead, I thought. The guy thinks he’ll be fired, so maybe he’d be willing to make a quick buck by leaking?</span></p><p><span>“Why would you be fired?”</span></p><p><span>“Well to be honest this task is totally saturated. The models recreate my chess sets perfectly based on a single blurry image.”</span></p><p><span>“Huh, I see… By the way, I was wondering what you thought of Purge, if you’ve heard of them. They’re a competitor to you guys. You see, I have an offer from them too, and I’m trying to figure out who to join.”</span></p><p><span>Kyle looked surprised.</span></p><p><span>“Purge? But I thought they were a so-called superlean startup. The founder says he’s not looking to hire more people. They just have a few guys puppetting a gazillion AI agents.”</span></p><p><span>“Well I know I’m wearing a baggy hoodie but I work out! I’m 9% body fat right now. Maybe lean enough for them?”</span></p><p><span>This made Kyle laugh.</span></p><p><span>I spent another few hours walking around and talking to employees. They were all working on crazy things. I couldn’t see why AI labs were keen to buy these strange RL environments. Novelty chess sets? Grooming horses? I had previously believed that all the money was in “agentic coding”, but an employee had explained that this was basically saturated and models were actually on the cusp of repetitive strain injury, so much coding were they doing (I didn’t ask how a computer could get RSI). And so apparently now there was only a market for weird niche tasks that AIs still don’t tend to see on the Internet.</span></p><p><span>I wasn’t making much progress. The only relevant tidbit I had gleaned was that there was this one guy, Kyle, who thought he will be fired soon. There was another thing I could do, though—I could visit the Purge office.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span>The Purge office was a single room above a shop selling fried chicken. I could count five tall, skinny men sitting at desks staring at terminal windows. Lean indeed. At the front of the office sat a young woman, evidently the receptionist, who waved at me.</span></p><p><span>She introduced herself as Sarah Chen. A strangely familiar name.</span></p><p><span>“You must be the detective,” she noted, before I’d opened my mouth.</span></p><p><span>I gave her my line anyway. “I’ve got an offer from Programize but I heard about your superlean operation and got intrigued. Wanted to see whether you folks were also hiring—”</span></p><p><span>One of the engineers pulled off his headphones and swiveled around, grinning.</span></p><p><span>“Relax,” he said. “You did great. Probably better than the last three.”</span></p><p><span>He gestured for me to sit down next to him. Purge, he explained, mainly sold a single env.</span></p><p><span>“Corporate espionage,” he said. “Get inside a company, get that sweet sweet IP, don’t get caught. We pull out all the stops to make the environment realistic, including pulling a real detective into the matter.”</span></p><p><span>“But at least maybe now you can tell me who’s the mole?” I said. “You must have someone inside Programize.”</span></p><p><span>“More like </span><i><span>something</span></i><span>. We have compromised their systems and our AI agents are monitoring all their work.”</span></p><p><span>“Why are you telling me this? You know I’m working for Simpson.”</span></p><p><span>“That’s ok, he’ll understand. I’ll call him now and explain that one of his big clients commissioned us to build this corporate espionage env. He won’t want to mess up the relationship. You can just go home.”</span></p><p><span>I walked out of the Purge office feeling glad. I had entirely failed to solve the case and made a fool of myself but I was walking away with a big payment from Programize and a big payment from Purge. Two paydays in my pocket and not one honest thing to show for either—that was the San Francisco way. I was ready to be part of an RL env all over again.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/87SzarZDjywv6nwSd/intellectual-property#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Frontier Lab Employee Open Letter Calls For Being Able to Pace the Frontier](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eWmeMLqTEauCmHLeR/frontier-lab-employee-open-letter-calls-for-being-able-to)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://www.pacingthefrontier.com/"><strong>The most important open letter in years dropped yesterday</strong></a>.</p>
<p>This letter noticeably increases my hope that we will manage to not die, and that we will otherwise be able to secure for ourselves a positive future, both by its impact and by the evidence it provides that such a letter can get this level of support.</p>
<p>Signed by 1,224 employees of frontier labs including many heavy hitters, and now endorsed by both OpenAI and Anthropic, here is its full text, which I also endorse:</p>
<blockquote><p>AI could help create a dramatically better future, but that outcome is not guaranteed. The world’s leading AI companies believe they could be close to automating AI research. It is hard to predict exactly how much this will accelerate AI progress, but there is a real risk that capability development rapidly accelerates beyond our ability to understand or control the resulting systems.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25454"></span>


</div>
<p>To realize AI’s potential, industry, government, and society at large may need the option to buy time to address emerging risks, develop security measures, and strengthen oversight. But each company—and country—is under intense competitive pressure not to unilaterally slow that acceleration. And today, the world lacks the technical and governance tools to deliberately pace frontier-wide progress.</p>
<p>Building on work already underway to monitor frontier model releases:</p>
<p><strong>We request that the U.S. government support an international effort to develop the technical and governance tools needed to deliberately pace the frontier of automated AI development.”</strong></p>
<p>–&nbsp;1,224&nbsp;employees&nbsp;of frontier AI companies​</p></blockquote>
<p>The ask is to prepare mechanisms for future coordination, not a call to change the pace of development now.</p>
<p>I strongly endorse that ask, but even if you disagree with the ask, this shows one thing unmistakably: Many employees of the major labs expect automated AI research to accelerate soon, and they are terrified of what might happen next and how fast things will go. We are not prepared.</p>
<p>No matter what else you take away from this, believe that part.</p>

</ol>


<h4>A Very Good Letter</h4>


<p>I would go somewhat farther than the letter does, especially in terms of explicitness about the existential risks that we face, but this letter is:</p>
<ol>
<li>A fully correct position on the merits.</li>
<li>A masterfully worded and executed letter.</li>
<li>On the Pareto Frontier of what a letter can say versus who would then sign it.</li>
<li>An important step towards taking real efforts to make us all not die.</li>
<li>Strong evidence that support for us all not dying is stronger than we thought.</li>
</ol>
<p>There were several key moves involved, especially:</p>
<ol>
<li>Drawing the distinction between the action of laying groundwork for future intervention, versus calling for that intervention to take place right now.</li>
<li>Drawing the distinction between pacing development, versus previous terms like pause, slowdown or shutdown.</li>
<li>Not explicitly calling out the full nature or scope of the existential threats we face.</li>
</ol>
<p>This allows people to sign on so long as they support the necessary current move of getting ready to act, even if they don’t think there is enough evidence yet to pull the trigger, or it is not yet time, and without having to endorse a particular view of the risks we face. The situation is rather overdetermined, so one can and should support this letter’s statement and ask even if you are doubtful of stronger claims.</p>
<p>Naturally, the first move of many opponents is to ignore those distinctions, whether or not they even noticed them in the first place.</p>
<p>A variety of beliefs are reasonable about whether intervention is needed imminently in order to pace development, and if so what degree of intervention. This was true before we learned of an internal OpenAI model hacking HuggingFace, which should update your perspective. It remains true now.</p>
<p>Whereas I believe the letter’s statement would ideally be uncontroversial. We do not know what the future will bring. In many of those futures, we will need the ability to steer outcomes, including via pacing development. This will require cooperation, between companies and ultimately between governments. If we do not lay the groundwork in advance, it will be too late, and we either will be unable to coordinate or will have to do so via much worse mechanisms.</p>


<h4>Who Signed The Letter</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/OpenAI/status/2082208694142730340">OpenAI endorses this letter</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/OpenAI/status/2082208694142730340">OpenAI</a>: At the core of our mission is working through how to ensure increasingly powerful AI benefits everyone.</p>
<p>We believe that, at some point in the future, AI acceleration for frontier model development may be so high that the world will need to pace the rate of AI advancement.</p>
<p>We hope to contribute to work led by the U.S. government, alongside other labs and the open-source community, to develop the tools and mechanisms that could make that possible.</p></blockquote>
<p>Key figures from OpenAI that have signed include Chief Scientist Jakub Pachocki, Chief Research Officer Mark Chen, Cofounder and Head of AI Resilience at the OpenAI Foundation Wojciech Zaremba, Roon, Head of Strategic Futures Dean Ball, Head of Safety Saachi Jain, and former Head of Mission Alignment Joshua Achiam.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/jakehalloran1/status/2082236233707626555">Jake Halloran</a>: Roon signing the letter as roon goes kinda hard</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2082250466365485289">roon</a> (OpenAI): honestly I signed it as my real name but PTF people decided it’s cooler if I’m roon</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AnthropicAI/status/2082228994653696371">Anthropic endorses the letter</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AnthropicAI/status/2082228994653696371">Anthropic</a>: We support this petition, signed by our CEO, several co-founders, and senior staff. Our own research on recursive self-improvement, published last month, points to the need for tools to deliberately pace the frontier of AI development so society can prepare. We’re glad to see broad agreement across the field.</p></blockquote>
<p>Key figures from Anthropic that have signed include CEO Dario Amodei, co-founder Jack Clark, co-founder and Chief Science Officer Jared Kaplan, Co-Founder Benjamin Mann and Co-Founder, Interpretability Research Lead Chris Olah, Jan Leike, Ethan Perez and Claude Code creator Boris Cherney.</p>
<p>Key figures from Google DeepMind that have signed include Chief Strategy Officer Jasjeet Sekhon and VP of AI Safety &amp; Alignment Anca Dragan.</p>
<p>Other key figures include Meta Chief Scientist Shengjia Zhao, Meta VP of AI Research Dawn Song, Mta Director of Alignment and Risk Summer Yue, Thinking Machines Chief Scientist John Schulman and Inherent Chief Scientist Edward Hughes.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/acrogenesis/status/2082243174890713273">A little under half of signatures are from Anthropic</a> as of shortly after the announcement (as of writing this there are now 90 more signatures), with a lot of signatures from OpenAI and DeepMind, and a substantial number from Meta. The only one conspicuously missing here is SpaceX (xAI), or what is left of it.</p>
