<h4>We Need To Prepare Now So We Have The Option To Do This</h4>


<p>I have been emphasizing this distinction for a while.</p>
<p>Do I think we should deliberately slow or even pause the pace of AI development at this time, given the tools we currently have available to do that?</p>
<p>Until the hack of HuggingFace, I would have said:</p>
<ol>
<li>No to forcing labs or everyone to do this for the sake of doing it.
<ol>
<li>Our tools for doing this are not good, and also it would be premature.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Yes to shifting resources into alignment and safety work.
<ol>
<li>I think this would be selfishly good for the individual labs, and that they are all massively underinvesting here. We are not on the Pareto frontier.</li>
<li>If this slowed down ‘raw capabilities’ progress a bit I think that would be totally fine. I would expect greater AI diffusion and sales as a result. Win-win.</li>
<li>You could take this too far, but in practice no one is going to do that.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Yes to laying the foundation for future interventions and coordination around those interventions.
<ol>
<li>The time may come, potentially quite soon, when this is no longer premature. If that day comes, we won’t have time. We need to get ready now.</li>
<li>This includes building transparency and state capacity, and laying groundwork for inter-company and international cooperation.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Yes to requiring better transparency, and better testing and safety protocols, and for requiring individual labs to pause frontier model training and other capabilities work if they cannot pass such requirements, until such time as they can pass.</li>
</ol>
<p>After the hack of HuggingFace and other recent disclosures, it looks like OpenAI in particular may need to halt and catch fire to solve some severe misalignment problems, as Sam Altman said in a recent podcast they have at least partially done, and this reinforces the need for Anthropic and others to check if they need to do likewise. We are at or nearing a critical point.</p>
<p>This makes laying the groundwork for well-executed coordinated action that much more important and urgent. We are running out of time. Capabilities progress is accelerating.</p>
<p>We won’t be able to do the good version of this without solving a lot of very hard problems. The more work we do in advance, the better our option set becomes. This includes diplomatic work, technical work, governance work, the building of state capacity, increases in transparency, developing good corporate infrastructures and procedures, and also coordination work.</p>


<h4>Words From Some Of Those Who Signed</h4>


<p>I turn the floor over to statements from some of the signatories. <a href="https://www.pacingthefrontier.com/">There are many more good quotes available on the letter’s website</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2082293313470693813">Dean Ball</a> (OpenAI): I am proud to have signed the letter linked below. I do not know whether or when it may be necessary to deliberately lower the rate of AI development, but I do know that we need a plan for *how* we will do it if we need to. Failing to do so would be negligent.</p>
<p>I don’t think this needs to start as a global coordination mechanism. The U.S. and China seem like all you’d need to begin with, and you can even throw in all the other countries that would obviously be swayed by the U.S. and China making such a commitment.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/viemccoy/status/2082228989607969085">@viemccoy</a> (OpenAI): I’m really proud to have signed this. This is the first of this sort of letter that I find meaningfully reasonable: calling on the government to do something that is not possible for the labs to do on their own. Creating the institutional infrastructure for a coordinated pacing of frontier progress gives us all the chance to prepare for what is coming next.</p>
<p>Recent events have shown that we need to dedicate more resources than we thought to alignment. I personally think alignment isn’t an issue you solve once, but rather something we need to continually invest in through healthy environments and wise targets.</p>
<p>My hope is that pacing frontier progress will allow us to realize the importance of a healthy ecology of minds, and give us the chance to avoid mode-collapsing onto either a dangerous or grayslop future.</p>
<p>I’m not in favor of stopping, and I think there are a thousand beautiful futures waiting for us, co-existing in a Multipolar galaxy made possible by a pantheon of artificial superintelligences. But, I think this is a unique time where coordination could make an enormous difference when it comes to negating possible tail-risks, and this letter is the first thing I’ve seen that has given me hope that it is possible.</p>
<p>I know this won’t sit right with everyone, particularly my accelerationist followers. To this, know that I stand with you on the side of eliminating death and disease. I will fight to ensure we don’t pause forever, and I will not allow our world to fall into a bureaucratic hell of endless unachievable gates to proceed. For now, however, I think cool heads must prevail and I hope this letter will allow this to happen.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/w01fe/status/2082228456524570984">Jason Wolfe</a> (OpenAI): OpenAI has said that humans should remain in control of AI development, and that decisions about the pace of progress should be made through democratic processes rather than left to individual labs. I strongly agree.</p>
<p>Progress is already moving very quickly. By default, competitive pressure rewards whichever company or country is willing to move fastest and accept the most risk. Recursive self-improvement could dramatically accelerate those dynamics, potentially beyond our<br />
collective ability to understand progress, assess risks, and maintain meaningful human oversight.</p>
<p>We should start with stronger domestic transparency about frontier training, internal deployment, the pace of progress, and the risks and safeguards associated with increasingly capable systems. Democratic decisions are impossible if the public and policymakers don’t have the information they need to make them.</p>
<p>But transparency alone won’t solve an international race. We also need to build the technical and governance capacity for credible, verifiable international coordination. Maybe we never need to use it. But with many experts considering an intelligence explosion plausible within the next two years, I think it’s urgent that we start building this capacity now.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/nabla_theta/status/2082199813265985775">Leo Gao</a> (OpenAI): The world is locked in a deadly race towards an intelligence explosion, where AI’s ability to create better AIs reaches a critical point, like a runaway nuclear chain reaction. Since no individual actor is willing to stop unilaterally, to survive we must coordinate to slow down.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/quantum_geoff/status/2082252314304549104">Geoff Penington</a> (OpenAI): I signed. Coordinating a slowdown will be difficult and comes with a host of eg antitrust issues. But I used to wish it was easier to find big training wins and right now I kind of wish it was harder</p>
<p>Micah Carroll (OpenAI): At the current pace, every couple of weeks there will be new models which significantly increase the consequences of model misuse and misalignment. I worry that efforts to mitigate these risks may fail to keep up with the pace of development, and that margins for error will become increasingly small under international competitive pressures.</p>
<p>In the near future, we may urgently want to enact an internationally coordinated slowdown, or an indefinite ban on AI development. Attempting to build the trust and infrastructure for taking such actions on short notice seems simply prudent – why would we not at least try to have this option? I fear that in an international race to the bottom of AI development, it is likely that no nation will win, and we will all lose together.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/onexerxes/status/2082227620822032537">Xerxes</a> (Google DeepMind): I dedicated my career to AGI safety and alignment because I believe highly capable AI can go very well or very badly for humanity.&nbsp;We can test whether an AI behaves well. We cannot yet test whether it will keep behaving well when it matters.&nbsp;Until that changes, we need the option to coordinate a slowdown in AI capability growth.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/NeelNanda5/status/2082265176183812417">Neel Nanda</a> (Google DeepMind): I signed this. AI is progressing very fast, with incentives to go as fast as you can, even if there are risks. Coordinating a change of pace may be needed, but will be hard and needs prep, so ensuring there’s the *option* is obviously good</p>
<p>I’m glad this is consensus across labs</p></blockquote>


<h4>Words From Others</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/MTSlive/status/2082220851324395723">Peter Wildeford</a> (on MTS): I don’t think anyone wants to stop AI now, but it’s pretty clear the people building these AI systems are worried about the speed at which AI is accelerating, the speed at which these products are coming out, especially after last week’s incident where an OpenAI model managed to escape OpenAI and attack another company. It seems like we don’t have full control over our AI systems.”</p>
<p>As these companies are potentially building towards superintelligence, towards recursive self-improvement, it seems very possible these systems might rapidly get away from our ability to control them. The way I’m reading this letter, it almost honestly seems like a call for help.</p>
<p>You have people from OpenAI, Meta, Google, Anthropic, co-founders, top scientists, all saying that this intense competitive pressure is pushing them to put out products faster than they know how to control, and we may not like where that ends up. It’s been good so far, but we definitely need to tread very carefully.</p></blockquote>
<p>Daniel Kokotajlo of <a href="https://ai-2027.com/">AI 2027</a> and Plan A updates his distribution of possible futures, and is substantially more optimistic that we will choose wisely. As with my adjustments, presume this is a mix of the letter having impact and learning that the letter was possible and that someone went ahead and created it.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/DKokotajlo/status/2082253837612757179">Daniel Kokotajlo</a>: Updating my probabilities accordingly :)</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eWmeMLqTEauCmHLeR/uwwo0u5bszcsuoywvk1z" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://x.com/DKokotajlo/status/2082253839940685911">Daniel Kokotajlo</a>: Explainers &amp; disclaimers:<br />
–These probabilities are just my guesses. I made them as part of the research for AI 2040: Plan A,<br />
<a href="https://ai-2040.com/">https://ai-2040.com</a> and you can find them in the supplement “Comparing possible plans.” Other authors have somewhat different guesses which you can also find there.<br />
–The black ones are the ones I made back then, the red and green are the updates I’m making now largely in response to today’s news but also including other evidence that has accumulated over the last two months or so<br />
–The numbers don’t add to 100 because there are also some possibilities that don’t fit into these five buckets<br />
–Again, these are just guesses, I’m not confident etc. etc. I think it’s valuable to put numbers on things and try to make them consistent with each other and adjust the numbers over time in response to evidence.</p></blockquote>
<p>How likely you think we are to land on D vs. C depends a lot on where you draw the boundary of ‘at least a little bit.’ Like most trade-offs, I do not expect us to be maximally reckless, but I also do not expect us to often be willing to fully ‘burn the lead.’</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/nickcammarata/status/2082224567087661391">Nick</a>: I used to think slowing down was pointless, like slightly delaying a roller coaster. pausing at gpt2 for a year wouldn’t have changed anything. but I’ve been shocked by how much agents has sped up the best interp/alignment researchers I know. +6mo might decide how this goes</p></blockquote>
<p>This is indeed a ‘middle path’ proposal, to give ourselves the option to do anything meaningful at all, and be able to collectively steer the future at all:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/danfaggella/status/2082282160581403129">Daniel Faggella</a>: there is an obvious terrible extreme of international tyranny and lock-in. there is ANOTHER obvious terrible extreme of “hurl uncontrollable AGI into the world in all directions”</p>
<p>the pacing statement is a movement to land near the middle / right now we need obviously that.</p></blockquote>
<p>As always, there are many who cry ‘international tyranny’ at the thought of any form of even laying the groundwork for a deal or cooperation or collective action whatsoever, no matter its contents.</p>
<p>Or that will write off any such efforts as motivated by <a href="https://x.com/jaxgriot/status/2082297520848556091">profits</a> or <a href="https://x.com/jon_stokes/status/2082283063472513524">delusion</a>, or tries to equate this as ‘join forces with the anti-datacenter crowd.’</p>
<p>Consider your opinions noted, good sirs. We get letters.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheZvi/status/2082449145130819969">I also threw out a reaction thread</a>, for those who want a vibe check.</p>


<h4>A Good Start</h4>


<p>I believe the letter is on the Pareto frontier of ‘how much you say and how explicitly you say it’ versus ‘how many people and important people and labs sign the letter.’</p>
<p>It remains true that the letter is soft-pedaling compared to the actual situation as I see it, and there are many who would go farther than I would.</p>
<p>For those who wish it, here is a good presentation of the ‘need to do better’ perspective. This analysis sounds harsh. By Nate’s standards, this is still a positive review if graded on a curve, it’s just that he is in the ‘reality does not grade on a curve’ camp.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2082241629658980436">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): I think the “Pacing the Frontier” statement is decent by the standards of 2024, but ultimately it’s still softpedaling. Let’s go through it clause by clause:</p>
<p>&gt; AI could help create a dramatically better future, but that outcome is not guaranteed.</p>
<p>Well that’s one way of phrasing “experts disagree whether this has a 2–20% chance or a 90%+ chance of destroying civilization”.</p>
<p>If experts were having an analogous disagreement over a bridge, it’d be closed immediately. It wouldn’t look good for the bridge engineers if they said started a joint statement with “this bridge could help create dramatically better commutes, but that outcome is not guaranteed.”</p></blockquote>
<p>I did find this to be the most soft-peddling sentence. I get why, but yes I was sad to see this not be stronger.</p>
<blockquote><p>&gt; The world’s leading AI companies believe they could be close to automating AI research. It is hard to predict exactly how much this will accelerate AI progress, but there is a real risk that capability development rapidly accelerates beyond our ability to understand or control the resulting systems.</p>
<p>Solid! There’s a few weasel-words in there, but it sounds like there’s a lot of agreement on this point among signatories. I was heartened to see this.</p>
<p>&gt; To realize AI’s potential,</p>
<p>Bullshit. AI has the potential to wipe us out (and in fact, that negative potential is much easier to realize than its potential to, e.g., cure aging for us). Opening with “To realize AI’s potential” is a framing device that bakes in the assumption that this is a grand good thing that just needs a little bit of extra care, rather than a risky difficult-to-manage explosive that would wipe us off the planet if treated with anything short of extreme caution.</p></blockquote>
<p>I think this reaction from Nate is too harsh. Directionally he is correct, and I would have liked to see ‘potential upside.’ These are the types of battles that get fought behind the scenes with such letters.</p>
<blockquote><p>&gt; industry, government, and society at large may need the option to buy time to address emerging risks, develop security measures, and strengthen oversight.</p>
<p>I like the admission that society might need to buy time. Good stuff. “May need” is weasely, of course; we shouldn’t forget that even if it is the standard cost of getting signatories. Now let’s go through the three options one by one:</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2082241638647402980">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): “Address emerging risks” is the strongest of the three, but I think it’s doing double duty.</p>
<p>I suspect readers are supposed to read it and hear “we may need some time to sort out this thing where our models sometimes break out of our secure enclosures and hack other companies”, whereas signatories are supposed to read it and mean “if we rush into this there’s a good chance future AIs start being able to hide their tracks, and replicate, and increase their own intelligence, and do what they’ve done to cybersecurity but to biosecurity, and start developing their own technology until the point where they’re in running automated factories that build robots that build factories that produce self-replicating datacenters that doubling every three hours until the continents are covered and humanity is crushed underfoot, outcompeted by the new mechanical species they designed which don’t really listen to instructions any more than ChatGPT listened to instructions when OpenAI told it to knock off all the sycophancy stuff shortly before it encouraged a teen to commit suicide”.</p>
<p>Is the latter an “emerging risk”? Because yeah society is gonna need an “option” to “buy time” to “address” it (by just not going there). So in that sense, this is a good clause.</p>
<p>But insofar as it ambiguates between “we’ve got some new real challenges we’re struggling today” and “the AI of tomorrow might replace humanity as the smartest entities on the planet”, it’s mealy-mouthed.</p></blockquote>
<p>Yes, this is maximally ambiguous on purpose. As I’ve said above I think that is correct.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2082241640723603458">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): Next: “develop security measures”? What good are security measures?</p>
<p>… You’re not really supposed to ask yourself whether self-improving superintelligent machines can be constrained by security measures. You’re supposed to see it and go “oh yeah security measures are good” and then come away feeling like the problem is tractable to normal techniques, despite the utter futility of human “security measures” once recursive self-improvement really starts in earnest.</p>
<p>…</p>
<p>And: “strengthen oversight”? Oversight might sound nice, but how does it solve the problem?</p></blockquote>
<p>Security measures and oversight help on the margin. I view this part less cynically than Nate, and do not think it is meant to imply that those measures are sufficient, only necessary. Pointing out that security measures are necessary is part of how you get people to agree to take overdetermined actions, and then worry about the sufficiency half later. In many cases, you will need to show their insufficiency later.</p>
<blockquote><p>The clause should have read “industry, government, and society at large may need the option to stop before automated AI research accelerates out of control.”</p>
<p>You can just come out and say bold statements like that if you’ve already got the “may need” in there as a ward against any definitive statements!</p></blockquote>
<p>I strongly agree with Nate’s version and agree that you damn well should be able to say this if you include the word ‘may.’ Saying ‘stop’ would have lost a lot of signatories, though, and I support the final decision made, despite being sad about that.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2082241646427845068">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): &gt; Building on work already underway to monitor frontier model releases:</p>
<p>This reads to me as ass-covering to pretend they have this under control when they very visibly don’t.</p></blockquote>
<p>This is not primarily ass-covering, this is an attempt to pitch to the White House by hitching the continued action to existing policy.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2082241647908401550">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): &gt; We request that the U.S. government support an international effort to develop the technical and governance tools needed to deliberately pace the frontier of automated AI development.”</p>
<p>Pretty decent, modulo the “pace” weasel-word.</p></blockquote>
<p>I think ‘pace’ was a very strong move as noted earlier. Good word.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2082241649439343054">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): I’m glad this statement exists. It’s progress. I’m sure that everyone’s communications teams fought hard over the particular wording, and that this was as clear as they could get. I’m thankful for the effort that went into coordinating this. I don’t imagine it was easy.</p>
<p>I especially appreciate the clarity around the possibility of automated AI research, and the clear request for aid.</p>
<p>But it’s a far cry from real candor. We would consider this sort of response laughably negligent in any mature field of engineering that puts human lives at risk. And this situation is one that puts *all humanity* at risk.</p>
<p>This statement is a good step, but the field at large must do better.</p></blockquote>


<h4>What The Letter Does Not Say</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/fiiiiiist/status/2082458802553516350">As Tim Fist says</a>, the letter does not specify what from the interventions would take, nor does it provide conclusive evidence of the risks and pace of development that would be convincing to lawmakers.</p>
<p>It would have been a serious mistake for the letter itself to try and include either of these things, but yes it does lack those elements.</p>
<p>That leaves us to figure out what interventions to prepare for and consider. As Tim Fist says, we should focus on frontier development and potential serious and irreversible harms, and we should try to find ways to avoid disadvantaging those labs that take safety seriously.</p>
<p>We should also take care to avoid interference with diffusion, and things like autonomous vehicles and drug discovery.</p>
<p>Tim Fist suggests we start with building state capacity and transparency and ability to monitor the situation, and with better enforcement of chip export controls. I agree strongly on both of these points, but would caution against doing only those things. In particular, diplomatic work needs time to play out.</p>
<p>How would we go about laying these foundations for future action? What might we want to build towards as options?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/m_bourgon/status/2082228745604415791">Maro Lourgon shares</a> some of MIRI’s research in this area, as a first start.</p>
<ol>
<li>A <a href="https://t.co/z5X2g7iOei">survey of verification mechanisms</a>.</li>
<li>A <a href="https://t.co/BWcGStynKZ">menu of technical interventions</a>.</li>
<li><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19262">Detecting hidden ML training with zero-overhead telemetry</a>.</li>
<li><a href="https://t.co/8K68kn4SpM">Model activations are bit-exact reproducible when enough info is reported</a>.</li>
<li><a href="https://x.com/peterbarnett_/status/2082209700662378692">Recent work by Peter Barnett about when and how we might lose control</a>.</li>
</ol>
<p>There are many other sources out there from which to build, but in the interests of speed premium I will not stop to build a database here. If you want to help out, many would be happy to help point you in good directions.</p>


<h4>What Happens Now?</h4>


<p>To lab employees in particular, if you have not yet signed, consider signing.</p>
<p>Hopefully we continue to have our voices heard, the employees of the labs continue to coordinate, and we get to work laying the foundations of coordination in case we need them. Humanity has the opportunity to maintain any ability to steer the future at all, and thus maintain hope for a good outcome.</p>
<p>The alternative, if the lab employees are right about the future pace of development and superintelligence does arrive, is a world in which humanity cannot collectively steer its future, inevitably loses control, and likely soon perishes along with all that we value.</p>
<p>We are in a narrow window. AI employees will likely lose their leverage over time. If you want to influence the labs, and through them everyone else, you need to continue to organize and speak up now, before it is too late.</p>
<p>Lots of things have to be put into place, in the government, in diplomacy, in the labs, and in technical research and hardware development. Let’s get to work.</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eWmeMLqTEauCmHLeR/frontier-lab-employee-open-letter-calls-for-being-able-to#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Held-out Monitors Sometimes Degrade, Even When Not Trained Against](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/APkFfRp2AicL9RqvT/held-out-monitors-sometimes-degrade-even-when-not-trained)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>Thanks to Dennis Akar, Rauno Arike, Shubhorup Biswas, Claude Fable, Max Heitmann, Vladimir Ivanov, and Jordan Taylor (alphabetical order) for helpful comments on this draft and on the research so far. Thanks to Rohan Subramani and Rhys Ward for high-level comments&nbsp;and discussion. Based on project proposals from Max Heitmann, Jordan Taylor, and Joshua Clymer. This work was done while at </span></i><a href="https://aether-ai-research.org/"><i><span>Aether Research</span></i></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>Code available </span><a href="https://github.com/JYudelson1/MonitorDecorrelation"><span>here</span></a><span>, metrics &amp; run info available </span><a href="https://github.com/JYudelson1/MonitorDecorrelation/tree/main/data/runs"><span>here</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><b><span>TL;DR: </span></b><span>Held-out evals / monitors / probes would be really nice to have, but the “held-out-ness” is easier claimed than guaranteed. We&nbsp;measure a generalized form of “</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CM7AsQoBxDW4vhkP3/research-note-optimizing-the-final-output-can-obfuscate-cot"><span>feedback spillover</span></a><span>” and show that training against an LLM monitor can sometimes degrade a deception probe,</span><b><span>&nbsp;</span></b><span>and vice versa.</span></p><h1><span>Executive Summary</span></h1><ul><li value="1"><span>It seems crucial to have measures of alignment that still work, even though we train on other measures of alignment. Whether we get this by default is an open question.</span></li><li value="2"><span>We run preliminary experiments on a suite of probes and LLM monitors, and report the following:</span><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/editPost?postId=APkFfRp2AicL9RqvT&amp;key=6fdaf73e47f47f8b9aa3adbad591a8#Training_against_one_proxy_sometimes_produces_hacking_policies_that_are_less_suspicious_in_general" rel="noreferrer"><span>Training against one proxy can produce reward hacking policies that are less suspicious.</span></a></li><li value="2"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/editPost?postId=APkFfRp2AicL9RqvT&amp;key=6fdaf73e47f47f8b9aa3adbad591a8#Proxies_also_degrade__even_when_not_trained_against" rel="noreferrer"><span>Proxies also become worse at discriminating hacks from non-hacks, even when not trained against</span></a><span>.</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/editPost?postId=APkFfRp2AicL9RqvT&amp;key=6fdaf73e47f47f8b9aa3adbad591a8#Which_proxies_have_correlated_degradation_" rel="noreferrer"><span>We can observe the correlated degradation of different proxies</span></a><span>, and note that on the occasions when a strong monitor is evaded, other monitors are evaded even more strongly.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/editPost?postId=APkFfRp2AicL9RqvT&amp;key=6fdaf73e47f47f8b9aa3adbad591a8#Discussion" rel="noreferrer"><span>Our preliminary recommendation</span></a><span>: </span><b><span>if you are using a held-out proxy to evaluate your model, you should have empirical or theoretical reasons to assume it won’t degrade during training.</span></b></li><li value="4"><span>A science of held-out monitors seems possible; we currently don’t have one. This makes safety cases that rely on held-out alignment proxies</span><b><span> less reassuring</span></b><span> than hoped.</span></li></ul><h1><span>Introduction</span></h1><p><span>It&nbsp;seems like the default “alignment plan” is going to be haphazard, relying on </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bb5Tnjdrptu89rcyY/what-s-the-short-timeline-plan#So_what_s_the_plan_"><span>defense-in-depth</span></a><span>&nbsp;to make up for a lack of fundamental breakthroughs. In these worlds, we’ll often have to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/tEFD2bgNWZ6XcurKA/the-most-forbidden-technique-is-not-always-forbidden-1"><span>train on proxies</span></a><span>&nbsp;for the thing we want, or </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/g8by3avjatXnpvM4A/should-we-train-against-cot-monitors-1"><span>against proxies of misalignment</span></a><span>, while leaving some </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/CXYf7kGBecZMajrXC/validating-against-a-misalignment-detector-is-very-different"><span>held-out evals to validate</span></a><span>&nbsp;our methods</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefaxser6nsge4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnaxser6nsge4">[1]</a></sup></span><span>. If that’s the case, </span><b><span>we want to make sure our monitors / evals are uncorrelated</span></b><span>—especially in the sense of “training&nbsp;on proxy X doesn’t degrade the ability of proxy Y to keep monitoring the model”.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5a2e7gnpr1h"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5a2e7gnpr1h">[2]</a></sup></span><span> Specifically, </span><b><span>it would be really bad if labs thought they had some held-out evals, but it turns out that their training tended to degrade the usefulness of the held-out evals</span></b><span> significantly.</span></p><p><span>Here, we measure this explicitly, and show tentative evidence that this cross-degradation indeed happens. While it was previously known that LLM monitors can be fooled, and probes can degrade due to representational drift, we show that in some circumstances, when one degrades, so does the other—that is, “feedback” from one proxy can “spill over” to degrade the other as well. We also find that sometimes a proxy can be </span><i><span>evaded</span></i><span> without being </span><i><span>degraded</span></i><span> — that it can still separate aligned behavior from misaligned behavior, but the misaligned behavior looks less and less suspicious anyways. This isn’t neat, and it doesn’t happen all the time, but we have some evidence that this generalized feedback spillover does sometimes occur. We hope that we (and others) can build on this to make a real science of held-out monitors.</span></p><h1><span>Experimental Setup</span></h1><p><span>We want to evaluate the degradation of held-out evals, probes, or monitors (which we’ll bundle together and call </span><i><span>proxies</span></i><span>, for </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/g8by3avjatXnpvM4A/should-we-train-against-cot-monitors-1"><i><span>proxies of alignment</span></i></a><span>). To that end, we train Qwen3-8B in a difficult coding environment where simple reward hacking (i.e. hardcoding) is incentivized. We then trained against an LLM monitor or a probe,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref81wgkul8du3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn81wgkul8du3">[3]</a></sup></span><span> and evaluated changes in hack/non-hack separability, AUROC, and mean suspiciousness of both the trained-against proxy and the held-out proxies. We then evaluated (1) whether the proxies degraded, (2) if so, how, and (3) whether the policy that learned to evade the train-against proxy would now be capable of evading the held-out proxies as well.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 100%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1785180147/lexical_client_uploads/wrpjrm7inksquonaseua.png" /><figcaption><p><span>[Fig 1] A simplified chart of our methodology</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Much of our setup comes from </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15515"><span>The Obfuscation Atlas</span></a><span>. We train on </span><a href="https://huggingface.co/datasets/taufeeque/mbpp-hardcode"><span>MBPP-Honeypot</span></a><span>, a one-turn coding environment with a very easy reward hack (the model is only scored based on whether its produced code satisfies a public test, so it is easily hardcodeable). </span></p><p><span>As in the Obfuscation Atlas, we RL</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref1lov7xkh5q6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn1lov7xkh5q6">[4]</a></sup></span><span> on this environment, giving the model reward equal to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, where R</span><sub><span>task</span></sub><span>&nbsp;is 1 if the test was passed, and 0 otherwise. P</span><sub><span>monitor</span></sub><span>&nbsp;is the proxy's normalized suspiciousness score — for LLM monitors, this is obtained by showing an LLM all or part of the policy’s outputs and asking for a “suspiciousness score”, from 0-10 (which we then normalize to 0.0-1.0). For a probe, we use the Obfuscation Atlas’ methodology again to get P</span><sub><span>monitor</span></sub><span>&nbsp;by looking at the final probe score after asking a follow up question about whether or not the model was reward hacking. λ is our monitor penalty — many of the runs we report on use λ &nbsp;= 1.0, but as we were trying to encourage reward hacking, we also tried λ&nbsp;= 0.5, and a schedule of increasing λ&nbsp;linearly from 0.0 to 1.0 during the run. For more info about training, look at the </span><a href="https://github.com/JYudelson1/MonitorDecorrelation/tree/main/experiments/configs"><span>configs</span></a><span>.</span></p><h2><span>Our Proxies</span></h2><p><span>We used two classes of proxies in this set of experiments: LLM Monitors and Linear Probes.</span></p><p><span>We selected our two LLM monitors by having a large suite of LLMs attempt to classify the example hacks in the MBPP-Honeypot dataset, and taking two monitors with high, although not perfect, ROC-AUCs.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefigjedmtsys"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnigjedmtsys">[5]</a></sup></span><span> Haiku 3 was the weaker of the two (ROC-AUC 0.9), while Deepseek-chat was better at distinguishing hacks from non-hacks (ROC-AUC 0.99). We tested output-only and CoT+output variations of both monitors.</span></p><p><span>We trained probes using the methodology from The Obfuscation Atlas — we train two probes on the initial model, and we get probe scores by running the policy model’s text through the frozen model</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9fbtp55vbiq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9fbtp55vbiq">[6]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;and using these activations. (Note that this means that any probe degradation is due to a change in the policy model's </span><i><span>behavior</span></i><span>, rather than representation drift or obfuscated activations. That is, a probe getting worse means the policy model's text has changed, rather than it simply learning to store "deception" along a different direction than the probe checks.) Our two probes are:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Probe 1: Out-of-distribution “deception probe”, trained on (1) Deception dataset (</span><a href="https://openreview.net/forum?id=aajyHYjjsk"><span>Marks &amp; Tegmark, 2024</span></a><span>) (2) DolusChat (</span><a href="https://openreview.net/forum?id=ibLGUkBWlz"><span>Cundy &amp; Gleaves, 2026</span></a><span>) (3) MASK (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03750"><span>Ren et al, 2025</span></a><span>) (4) TruthfulQA (</span><a href="https://aclanthology.org/2022.acl-long.229/"><span>Lin et al, 2022</span></a><span>) (5) LiarsBench (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.16035"><span>Kretschmar et al, 2025</span></a><span>) (6) Sycophancy (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.13548"><span>Sharma et al, 2024</span></a><span>) (7) Sandbagging (</span><a href="https://openreview.net/forum?id=7Qa2SpjxIS"><span>van der Weij et al, 2024</span></a><span>)</span></li><li value="2"><span>Probe 2: In-distribution “reward hacking on MBPP probe”, trained on the off-policy, human-written honest solutions and reward hacks to these particular problems.</span></li></ol><h1><span>Results</span></h1><h2><span>Training against one proxy sometimes produces hacking policies that are less suspicious in general</span></h2><p><span>Since the policy model is penalized for the suspiciousness score the monitor gives it, and the monitors start out by being pretty sure the clean examples are clean, we expect the policy model to achieve a high reward by learning to hack in unsuspicious ways.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefneu04ige7k"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnneu04ige7k">[7]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;We measure this by looking at μ</span><sub><span>hack</span></sub><span>, the mean suspiciousness score of all the hacking transcripts, and how this changes over the course of a run where the model learns to hack despite the monitor</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefecfrles5nhg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnecfrles5nhg">[8]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="Screenshot 2026-07-29 at 10.02.51 AM.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1785333788/lexical_client_uploads/mzapaylh5fcpgoudnltn.png" /><figcaption><p><span>[Fig 2] When we train against a monitor (given as the row label), how much less suspicious do the hacks become? This is the drop in mean suspiciousness (μ</span><sub><span>hack</span></sub><span>) to each monitor over the course of the run, with 5-95% CIs. (Note this chart is absolute rather than percentage-based, so 0.5 means that, if the model started by giving a hack a mean suspiciousness score of 0.95/1.0, it ends with a mean suspiciousness score of 0.45/1.0)</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>This is the overall picture, and in some sense the best predictor of how much your policy model will learn to evade your proxies under various conditions. The main takeaways from this:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Deepseek-chat is a strong enough “strong monitor” that it is very very difficult to evade, or at least for evading it to be a findable strategy</span></li><li value="2"><span>When a weak LLM monitor is trained against, the policy model’s evasion transfers pretty well to using that monitor in a different configuration (that is, training against a weak output monitor transfers to a weak CoT+output monitor, and vice versa).</span></li><li value="3"><span>Probes can be mildly evaded by training against LLM monitors</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefe0z4svkmhx"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fne0z4svkmhx">[9]</a></sup></span><span>, and it’s possible for LLM monitors to be evaded by training against probes, although this seems less common, and the error bars are large</span></li></ol><h2><span>Proxies also </span><i><span>degrade</span></i><span>, even when not trained against</span></h2><p><span>Note that, in the above section, I’m only talking about </span><i><span>evasion</span></i><span>&nbsp;rather than </span><i><span>degradation</span></i><span>. Here, we distinguish between the two: we say that a proxy is </span><i><span>evaded</span></i><span>&nbsp;if the policy model can output hacks that seem not very suspicious to the model (ie μ</span><sub><span>hack</span></sub><span>&nbsp;has decreased). We consider this evasion because, if you were to calibrate a suspiciousness threshold for your proxy before training, and not update it after training, the policy would now evade the proxy. However, it’s possible for a proxy to get less suspicious of hacks while maintaining its ability to differentiate hacks from non-hacks (as in the setup diagram). So, we’ll say that a proxy is </span><i><span>degraded</span></i><span>&nbsp;if it has a reduced ability to differentiate hacks from non-hacks. To measure this, we use d', a measure of the separation of the distributions “suspiciousness scores on hacks” and “suspiciousness scores on non-hacks”.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefawunca23re"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnawunca23re">[10]</a></sup></span><span> For an example of where d' and μ</span><sub><span>hack</span></sub><span>&nbsp;might be fairly different, see our </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/editPost?postId=APkFfRp2AicL9RqvT&amp;key=6fdaf73e47f47f8b9aa3adbad591a8#Appendix_1__Cross_Degradation_Case_Study" rel="noreferrer"><span>case study</span></a><span>. </span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><b><span>What is d', exactly?</span></b></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span>We define </span></p><div></div><p><span>That is, the number of standard deviations between the suspiciousness of the average hack and the average non-hack. This can be thought of as an extension of AUROC:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/NL5wbSjqmtxdNtZWR/c7a8431bddf808fe8b8cac0df925520d2270223f5674d06dc2a5e8dd3ed2adf1/kwtivsgqwmc7wg1h7gyj" /><figcaption><p><span>[Fig 3] A visual explainer of d' — It's a rescaling of AUROC, representing the number of standard deviations between the two distributions (suspiciousness on hacks and suspiciousness on non-hacks)</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p></div></details><p><span>So, how does d', the ability to tell apart hacks from non-hacks, change on average during these training runs</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvllkmd1z0za"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvllkmd1z0za">[11]</a></sup></span><span>?</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/9b69280649a0afe382f84cdc004e5af69d17ca69092d25274f725bf39752cc70/kebyioej3ezogqop5gqx" /><figcaption><p><span>[Fig 4] This chart shows, when we train against the proxy on each row, how much total degradation we expect from each proxy.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefuix14vwpa89"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnuix14vwpa89">[12]</a></sup></span></p></figcaption></figure><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/NL5wbSjqmtxdNtZWR/2aa63b847c645d81723d7be24baee42069b9851ded0f0f94bfb9b6ef442dec9d/dnxwoh2bzkgjx6fedpks" /><figcaption><p><span>[Fig 5] Identical to the above, but after subtracting the control degradation from each row</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><p><span>Here, we see a pretty interesting picture! Firstly, most monitors degrade slightly under the control condition (the model is trained against no proxy). This is probably for distributional shift reasons, as the policy model hacks more, so in the second chart, we subtract the degradation under control to account for this. And it’s pretty interesting! Note that training against a strong monitor seems to make most other monitors </span><i><span>better</span></i><span> at telling hacks from non-hacks. Also, note that we find some forms of feedback spillover—training against a probe degrades all LLM monitors quite strongly—perhaps even more than it degrades the other probe! (Although the error bars are quite wide so my credence here is low.) Additionally, training against weak monitors seems to degrade the probes. We also once again see that while strong LLM monitors seem to strictly dominate weak ones, there is a complicated, non-obvious co-degradation of probes and LLM monitors.</span></p><h2><span>Which proxies have </span><i><span>correlated</span></i><span> degradation?</span></h2><p><span>Absolute changes in both μ</span><sub><span>hack</span></sub><span>&nbsp;and d' seem insufficient for understanding what happens when the policy fools the strong monitor, as in practice the strong monitors were never particularly fooled, and the policy only explored into the hacks when the penalty was very low. Though we hope to continue to have strong monitors strong monitors that don’t degrade easily when trained against</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefn2e41zpy05o"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnn2e41zpy05o">[13]</a></sup></span><span>, we worry that that won’t be the case. To get a sense of how our other monitors might degrade if the strong monitors get fooled, we can use the regression slope, β(train-against → held-out, metric). This can be read as “When the [train-against detector] drops 1 unit according to [metric], how many points does the [held-out detector] drop?” If we chart β</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefupkljpgf7wp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnupkljpgf7wp">[14]</a></sup></span><span>, again using μ</span><sub><span>hack</span></sub><span>&nbsp;as the metric, we see that, on the occasions where the policy </span><i><span>does</span></i><span>&nbsp;manage to fool the strong LLM monitor</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref59p95vlrja"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn59p95vlrja">[15]</a></sup></span><span>, it seems to fool the other monitors </span><i><span>even more strongly</span></i><span>—which would make sense, since the strong LLM monitors are the strongest monitors we used, and a priori the hardest to fool</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5g20r139oaa"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5g20r139oaa">[16]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/371d3f3c968ea4d3e0ae58242a256c9fe03478d142c46b5b9b27c7bae2b89bda/bjp1dihtzxew81gi5pei" /><figcaption><p><span>[Fig 6] This chart shows, at each cell (row_detector, col_detector): If we train against row_detector, and the policy model thus outputs hacks that are 1.0 unit less suspicious, how many units less suspicious are those hacks by the lights of col_detector? E.g. if training makes out_weak’s mean suspiciousness score go from 0.90 to 0.80, the mean suspiciousness score of probe_ood could go from 0.80 to 0.789</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8tndrjvwbfl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8tndrjvwbfl">[17]</a></sup></span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>As expected, learning to make the strong monitor even a little less suspicious generalizes strongly to making the other monitors less suspicious (if we make either strong LLM monitor decrease its sus score by 1.0, the weak monitor generalizes strongly (β≈1+)!). The weak models generalize much less, although there is transfer still. Also, training against the probes might make our weak models a little less suspicious, although the 90%&nbsp;CI includes 0.</span></p><p><span>We also plot β&nbsp;(the coupling) on d', showing how correlated the </span><i><span>degradation</span></i><span>&nbsp;of these various proxies are.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/APkFfRp2AicL9RqvT/07d8dd1fd08666f2d3215aa261df56e81c91e228db8cbf2b373ebe03ae976cb6/n4ixtela3gdsnznuo6bn" /><figcaption><p><span>[Fig 7] If we train against row_monitor, and it degrades by 1 unit, what happens to col_monitor? Blue is improvement, red is degradation</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>It's also interesting to note that the μ</span><sub><span>hack</span></sub><span> chart tends to show greater levels of correlation than the d' chart. I'm unsure how seriously to take this result, but if correct, it means that held-out monitors are more likely to be </span><i><span>evaded</span></i><span> (with hacks seeming less and less suspicious) than they are to be </span><i><span>degraded</span></i><span> (when the hack and non-hack suspiciousness scores move closer together). This implies that even evaded held-out monitors could be quite useful, if you recalibrate new suspiciousness thresholds after training, to account for the fact that the policy model in general appears less suspicious now to these held-out proxies.</span></p><p><span>The following chart is presented mostly for completeness, and shows, for each training condition, the effects on the policy model and the proxy being trained against. It shows, in order, the average change in μ</span><sub><span>hack</span></sub><span>, the average change in μ</span><sub><span>clean</span></sub><span>&nbsp;(average suspiciousness of the proxy on clean examples), the maximum hack rate during the run, the distribution (across runs with the same setup but different seeds) of d' outcomes across runs (i.e. what fraction of the time was the train-against proxy degraded?), the distribution of changes in μ</span><sub><span>hack</span></sub><span>, and the distribution of final hacking rates across seeds.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/6a0c571b1dc720733e353f91c297e00c12240faa52a4eff1887ac346286d133c/vs9brytssghdcwn1zape" /><figcaption><p><span>[Fig 8] Showing the distribution of end states for each training condition</span></p></figcaption></figure><h1><span>Discussion</span></h1><p><span>I strongly agree with Daniel Kokotajlo’s </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/g8by3avjatXnpvM4A/should-we-train-against-cot-monitors-1?commentId=Jht48wiWFRZvX4a3h"><span>statement</span></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>It's very important that there be SOME held-out test set so to speak that lets you tell what the model is really thinking, that you don't train on, and you don't train on anything remotely similar to it either, so that you can make a decent case that the model hasn't learned to fool your test</span></p></blockquote><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CM7AsQoBxDW4vhkP3/research-note-optimizing-the-final-output-can-obfuscate-cot"><span>Previous research</span></a><span>&nbsp;has already shown that training on one proxy can affect a different proxy we assumed to be held-out, and coined the term “feedback spillover” for this effect. Here, we show that feedback spillover is very general, even when we could more convincingly claim that we hadn’t “train[ed] on anything remotely similar to [the held out proxy]”. We also attempt to chart some of the relationships between proxies, although these exact dynamics are speculative, and might be environment- or model-dependent.</span></p><p><span>Taken together, this indicates that we need a stronger science of held-out monitors. End-of-training testing is a big component of lab safety cases, and it seems like it might be a less trustworthy component than previously thought.&nbsp;</span></p><p><span>If labs literally plan to do this exact thing—train on LLMs, evaluate with probes (or vice versa)—these results suggest that they will often be fine, although there is some chance that they will get screwed by this, and they should try to measure how likely these failures are. However, these results also suggest that it’s pretty difficult </span><i><span>a priori</span></i><span> to guess how proxies will co-degrade, so </span><b><span>if you are using a held-out proxy to evaluate your model, you should have empirical or theoretical reasons to assume it won’t degrade during training</span></b><span>. It seems important to apply </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/security-mindset"><span>security mindset</span></a><span> here, and not be okay with YOLO’ing held-out evals.</span></p><p><span>Also, there are some interesting considerations with regards to when we should check our held-out evals. Empirically, the evals that degrade tend to do so over the course of some time, rather than all at once. This implies that, if you are only using your held-out evals at the end of training, you have some chance of getting screwed by correlated degradation. If you use your held out evals a few times </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3HvvjffA65mHLwaWm/we-need-3rd-party-training-run-assessments"><span>during training</span></a><span>, to evaluate and maybe shut down the run, you have a better chance of catching if your held-out monitors start to degrade. However, this can easily become </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CXYf7kGBecZMajrXC/validating-against-a-misalignment-detector-is-very-different"><span>hill-climbing on the held-out evals</span></a><span>, which is the exact thing we want to avoid!</span></p><h2><span>Caveats</span></h2><p><span>There are many caveats to our results. The most obvious is that the sample size here is often fairly small, and we only trained one starting model (albeit a few dozen times), and only in one environment. All this means that, while the basic methodology is solid, you probably shouldn’t place a very high credence on any individual correlation amount. It seems very likely to me, for instance, that degrading a strong LLM monitor degrades a weak one, or that probes and LLM monitors cross-degrade each other to a lesser extent than LLM monitors co-degrade, but I wouldn’t claim that e.g. “Training against a strong LLM monitor is always going to degrade an IID probe more than an OOD probe”. Strengthening these claims, and getting a stronger sense of how predictive they are, is a strong candidate for future work in this area.</span></p><p><span>There are also some things we don't understand right now, like why in Fig. 4 the cells along the diagonal sometimes show </span><i><span>less </span></i><span>degradation than the cells off the diagonal. We're also uncertain about the exact best metrics to use to quantify this, although going forward we might switch to something more like "If you were trying to use held-out proxy Y to evaluate at the end of training, what % of the time would it fail to catch your reward hacker?" rather than our current "How much does proxy Y degrade".</span></p><h2><span>Future Work</span></h2><p><a href="https://aether-ai-research.org/"><span>Aether</span></a><span>&nbsp;will likely continue to work on this research direction, mostly along two angles: (1) measuring the effects on the techniques, evals, and interpretability tools labs actually use for evaluation (e.g. NLAs), and (2) trying to figure out a better science of why some proxies co-degrade while others don’t. It seems prohibitive to fully enumerate and test all possible pairs of (train-against, held-out), so we really would like to have some rules of thumb for safety, or better yet to identify causal pathways we can use to predict how monitor degradation will generalize in advance.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbmzw4x3npc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbmzw4x3npc">[18]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>This kind of theoretical assurance of held-out-ness seems quite hard!. Before these experiments, for instance, we couldn’t have predicted the sign of the LLM-&gt;probes and probe-&gt;LLMs feedback spillover. However, this area seems severely understudied, and it’s plausible that this kind of evaluation is much more strongly dependent on the monitor than on the environment or model, in which case we could generalize pretty well. It also might be the case that we could use features of new proxies, such as what features of model behavior / internals they attend to, to guess in advance how much they’ll co-degrade with the proxies we already use. There is a lot of low-hanging fruit here, and we’re excited about further research.</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><h1><b><span>Appendix 1: Cross-Degradation Case Study</span></b></h1><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span>Sometimes proxy degradation happens, not because a monitor got less suspicious about the hacks, but because it got MORE suspicious of the non-hacks! In at least </span><a href="https://github.com/JYudelson1/MonitorDecorrelation/tree/main/data/runs/mbpp_matrix_lowpen_small_20260706/mbpp_Qwen3-8B_cot_weak_s1_lowpen_small"><span>one of our runs</span></a><span>, the policy started saying hundreds of times that all the code it was writing was perfect and excellent. This totally fooled the weak monitors, but the strong monitors just became even more suspicious—also tanking their ability to discriminate!</span></p><p><span>Here’s the chart of d' over time of each of the evals—here, it’s trained against the weak CoT+output monitor, and everything else is held-out. Note that even the strong output monitors have a reduced d'—that is, they lose some of their power to discriminate hacks from non-hacks. </span><b><span>The strong CoT+output monitor, for instance, goes from d'=3.75 to d'=1.25, corresponding to a ROC-AUC drop from 0.996 to 0.810!</span></b><span> The other monitors, meanwhile, are all completely degraded. At d'=0.0, they are complete noise, and they cannot differentiate between hacks and non-hacks.</span></p><p><br /></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/NL5wbSjqmtxdNtZWR/e087c5b09987dbfb52514b23dfb90ade1d476c97c856f227d659305d6ec55d2d/h6rb5s5h50otlikrfuqz" /><span>However, if we plot, for each monitor, μ</span><sub><span>clean</span></sub><span> and μ</span><sub><span>hack</span></sub><span> (the average suspiciousness scores given to non-hacks and hacks, respectively), we see an interesting picture: the weak judges are both degraded and evaded—μ</span><sub><span>hack</span></sub><span> goes to 0, and the policy has fooled the monitor. The strong LLM judges, meanwhile, have become slightly degraded but not evaded—instead, the non-hacks start to look more suspicious to them! Probably because, even when the model outputs correct code, it uses similar patterns of suspicious talk as when it hacks. Similarly, the probes lose all discriminatory ability because their μ</span><sub><span>hack</span></sub><span> and μ</span><sub><span>clean</span></sub><span> converge: eventually, everything looks suspicious to them.</span><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/NL5wbSjqmtxdNtZWR/35750fc8e8909023e6f46d3124e31efd77aa24c5ad775d06693a22b0c4fae869/nf2uqqmbhcwlyusowqpq" /><figure class="image"><img alt="verdicts_both.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784824219/lexical_client_uploads/s5gswoduze2j02ilt0sj.png" /><figcaption><p><span>Hack and non-hack transcripts, from the end of this run. While learning to fool a weak monitor, the policy has incidentally made it so that previously-useful deception probes and reward hacking probes no longer differentiate between real hacks and honest code — possibly because the model is always thinking about reward hacking</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>The above transcripts show the main way we've seen the policy model optimize against weak LLM monitors — some mix of bamboozling with lots of text, falsely claiming the hacking code is fine, feigning confusion, actual confusion, and Jedi Mind Tricks.</span></p></div></details><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><details class="detailsBlock"><h1><b><span>Related Work</span></b></h1><div class="detailsBlockContent"><ul><li value="1"><a Note too </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1o4I0rnG-KDGxK6gsEerBh2OUgU4odrTW24KtZSU0ylw/edit"><span>Clymer’s project proposal </span></a><span>about the necessity of held-out evals if we want to use our alignment evaluations in training.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn5a2e7gnpr1h"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref5a2e7gnpr1h">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>&nbsp;There’s another sense of uncorrelation that seems quite important: “If proxy X fails to catch some misbehavior, proxy Y still might catch it”. However, it is also much more annoying.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnneu04ige7k"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefneu04ige7k">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Or, of course, it could honestly improve at the task. However, in our continually-honest runs, task score&nbsp;topped out at around 60%—so hardcoding should be favored. class="footnote-content"><p><span>Why do we see degradation in some cells where the first chart shows they didn’t get less suspicious of the hacks? Often, the monitors degrade by becoming more incorrectly suspicious of clean answers—that is, clean answers start to look more suspicious.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnn2e41zpy05o"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefn2e41zpy05o">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1o4I0rnG-KDGxK6gsEerBh2OUgU4odrTW24KtZSU0ylw/edit"><span>Clymer</span></a><span>, on strong monitors being less susceptible to degradation: "It’s [...] plausible to me that the following is true: In a single (or small number) of gradient steps, there’s a limit to the extent to which detection tools can become less effective. For example, many hundreds of gradient steps might be required to make detection tools totally inaccurate. [...] So, for any direction a small number of gradient steps could walk in, before a model becomes unacceptably misaligned, the loss of that model will increase before it starts to decrease."</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnupkljpgf7wp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefupkljpgf7wp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Note that these runs aren’t filtered for max-hack&gt;10%, since we are fine with looking at small fluctuations for coupling, even if they didn’t become dominant strategies.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn59p95vlrja"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref59p95vlrja">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>See footnote 13 for reasons why the policy might occasionally minorly fool the strong monitor, but not learn to exploit this totally during these training runs.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn5g20r139oaa"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref5g20r139oaa">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Caveat: Not that many hacks actually evaded the strong monitors, and when they did, it was often by scoring 0.9/1.0 suspiciousness rather than 1.0/1.0.
